ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e141	The representative, Frank Bakke Jensen, will present a proposal for a representative.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e144	On behalf of the representatives Svein Flätten, Elisabeth Røbekk Nørve, Bjørn Lødemel and myself, I would like to make a representative proposal on a strategy for the Norwegian mining industry.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e149	The proposal will be dealt with in a regulatory manner.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e161	Foreign policy, too, is the focus of attention: new settlements in East Jerusalem, military offensive in South Afghanistan, earthquakes in Haiti and Chile, disarmament negotiations between the United States and Russia, cooperation meetings in the Arctic, a forthcoming EU summit; the list that fills the foreign policy agenda will be slightly long. But over the individual issues, at different times, there are relationships or events that define the gravity of foreign policy. In the decade we leave, the terrorist attack on the United States on 11 September 2001 appears as such. Challenges related to the national security of countries have dominated the outlook and behaviour of foreign and security policy during this decade. This will last as we go into a new decade. At the same time, new, heavy forces for change are emerging, which also have a decisive influence on foreign policy. Increasingly, we have changed living conditions and the struggle for resources as a result of climate change, the effects of the global economic crisis and the emergence of what we have so far called major developing countries as heavy and central actors in an increasingly globalised world economy. Over the last decade, we have seen how the financial crisis hit the real economy. It turned out to be global and had ring-effects in all corners of the world, with gradual consequences for all policy areas. Now, one year later, we see that this is still playing out. Although there are tangible signs that the world economy is resuming, we can see that the world is not going to be what it was. The financial crisis did not come alone either. In 2008, strong increases in global food prices caused major problems for the world's poor. The world needs concerted international efforts to increase the production of food — which is cheap enough — in the coming years. The financial crisis does not kill or harm as the attacks of 11 September 2001. The effects are more long-term, more profound, and they will be able to affect several countries, more people and challenge countries far beyond the experience of exposed physical security. The effects of the financial crisis illustrate what a wise man once said: big changes are coming sneaking on cat pots. Today, we can see that the effects of the financial crisis will be crucial for foreign and security policy in this decade. It also means that the context, the framework for dealing with all issues of foreign policy, will be changing. This will be the focus of my presentation today, which will not be a directory of all the individual matters. We have had thorough debates in this Chamber on Afghanistan, the North Region and human rights interpellations, and twice a year I have reports on developments in Europe – therefore, starting from the financial crisis. History gives us many lessons that economic crises are the source of upheaval, war and conflict. The deep crisis in the interwar period with its dramatic consequences is perhaps the most eloquent example. On the other hand, crises can also force necessary uprisings and pathways to rise. History has shown that lessons learned from crises can lead countries to take forward-looking measures to combat new crises. I'm thinking about how the Bretton Woods construction work was just aimed at preventing the 1930s' experience being repeated, or how the European Communities were built, based on the experience of the First and Second World Wars. So it's less the crisis itself than the way we're dealing with it, which is crucial. Behind our own time spectacular bankruptcies from Wall Street to Reykjavik lie deeper imbalances of several kinds. We see financial imbalances, between countries with large deficits and countries with high profits, where the United States particularly illustrates the first category and China the second. We see imbalances between poor and rich countries, we see imbalances between actual greenhouse gas emissions and the Earth's tolerance limit, we see political imbalances, between the increasingly global markets driven by deregulation and technological innovation, and the political power that remains largely linked to each country. International cooperation was crucial when it stormed in the autumn of 2008. The International Monetary Fund, the IMF, gained new relevance, and the G20 cooperation – the forum in which finance ministers and governors from 19 of the world’s largest economies and the EU meet – gradually grew as the main body. But first-line defense came in the United States and other countries that were hit early, through massive measures the first days and weeks after Lehman Brothers went bankrupt on 15 September 2008, in order to secure the financial system and save socially important financial institutions. The G20 meeting in London in April 2009 meant that the heaviest countries coordinated their national policies and agreed on the scope and arrangements for international action. They also agreed to provide the IMF and other international financial institutions with new financial resources to assist particularly severely affected Member States. Behind, and in parallel with the outbreak of the most serious financial crisis since the mid-war, there is a constant shift in regional and global power and influence. New countries report through population growth, production, technology and emissions. They sign up to secure their own development, fight poverty, meet climate challenges, be heard, seen and respected: China, India, Indonesia, Brazil, South Africa and more. The clearer they are, the clearer we see that established international institutions – created with western dominance in a context after the Second World War – are weakened both in representativeness and legitimacy. Our impression is reinforced by a disparity between the weights of the international and economic change forces and the lack of weight in the political institutions that can do something with them. All of these threads weave a complex backstop for reforms of the international system. It is difficult enough to reach agreement among so many actors with quite a different starting point – political, economic, cultural and religious. And it is particularly challenging when, at the same time, a large number of governments are placed on national responsibility to deal with the effects of the financial crisis here and now, with the effects it has on individuals, families and communities experiencing pressures against jobs and well-being. Because even in the global age, all policies are local. In the initial phase of the crisis, we feared that banks would go bankrupt. Now we're afraid that states like the same fate. For many governments, the framework for policies has changed significantly in a very short time, partly dramatically reducing freedom of action. as a result of growing deficits and heavy debt burden, but also as a result of the disappearance of important sources of income such as tourism and transfers from foreign workers. In many countries, which are also close to Norway, there may be a huge gap between demands, expectations and standard of living on the one hand and political ability to act on the other. All of this can happen quickly. What we have seen as solid national and international political structures can be disrupted in a short time. Once again, we can learn lessons from the 1930s. Then the economic downturn made the road shorter to authoritarian regimes and the appointment of scapegoats. Paradoxically, while the world really needs stronger regional and international bodies, individual countries, in dealing with their own crisis, may be forced to look inwards and oppose, or lack, the ability to build up the international governing bodies needed. The ability and willingness to take responsibility over one’s own country and its own population can be greatly reduced. Norway is also affected by the financial crisis. Our exports are affected by falling demand in key markets. The government prioritizes the work of keeping order in the Norwegian economy. We have an active policy to ensure the well-being, work and modernisation of Norwegian society. The OECD’s recent assessment is that the Norwegian response to the crisis has been well-designed and has had much to do with the desired effect. Our own national challenge is to take good care of this starting point – not least by preventing the rise in unemployment. In our foreign policy, the main step must be to continue our work on an international legal order with strong and adaptable international organisations that can cope with imbalances and help steer us out of them. The main conclusions of St.meld. No 15 for 2008-2009, and its treatment by the Storting: Norway shall and will take international responsibility, and Norway is served with the strongest possible international legal order. I would like to reflect on some areas that illustrate the impact of the financial crisis on international policy and on Norwegian foreign policy. Firstly, international cooperation. One of the main reasons we have so far avoided the depression of the 1930s is that we met the financial crisis with close multilateral cooperation. The countries had an institutional device in place and were willing to use it. Coordinated rescue operations lacking parallel in history have long prevented a collapse in the world economy. By means of the World Trade Organisation (WTO) multilateral trading system, we have so far also been able to avoid turning in a protectionist direction. Global legislation has helped to prevent short-term protectionist actions, despite domestic policy pressures for such measures in many countries. According to the WTO, the world economy is as open today as before the crisis, but it is warned that countries will feel the pressure to protect themselves against the packages of other countries through increased trade barriers – hidden and indirect. It is essential for Norwegian interests that this does not happen. Open world trade is essential for Norwegian interests. The Government will therefore continue to work in all relevant forums to counter increased protectionism. The question is whether the global spirit of cooperation from 2008 and 2009 will be maintained, as the most acute phase of the financial crisis is now over. The recovery is uneven. There is disagreement as to when the action packages are to be phased out and mitigation measures are phased in. The crisis makes sparks visible between surplus countries and deficit countries. For some countries, the social and political broad side of the crisis is only now reporting — with the effects of record deficits and record debt at the same time. The deficits must be reduced and the debt must be served. Overall, there may be a great burden on domestic policy. And in our world it also has foreign policy implications for the current state or groups of states' spaces of action. According to the IMF, China’s and other Asian currencies are undervalued and contribute to keeping prices of their exports artificially low. This affects export industries both in industrialised countries and in developing countries. In the Doha Round of developments in the WTO, there is still a lack of progress with uncertainty as to whether the United States is ready to conclude the negotiations, while much depends on whether the United States and China can reach an agreement. It has been almost ten years since these negotiations began. Much has been achieved, but nothing is clear until everything is clear. It will be difficult to get on with other important agendas for world trade unless the Doha Round comes into port, for example. important issues such as agreements that place a high focus on the environment and promote trade in environmentally friendly goods and services — including climate technology — or agreements that safeguard the rights of workers and the right to decent work. Those agendas are waiting for them – until Doha comes to an end. Norway will do its part to prioritize these issues and contribute to the swift conclusion of the Doha Round, and we will stick to our positions in the negotiations. Secondly, parallel and interwoven in the effects of the financial crisis change multilateral cooperation. The importance of the G20 illustrates the global shift of power that has taken place in the direction of Asia, and in particular China. We are witnessing a significant change in the conditions of power in international politics. The power shift started before the financial crisis broke out, but has been accelerated and reinforced. We see new alliances, led by China, India, Brazil, Indonesia, South Africa and others, building new cooperation relations – including in areas such as climate, development, health and human rights. Reality is clear: we cannot expect international solutions on crucial issues without the emerging economies sitting at the table. We saw it during the climate negotiations in Copenhagen, we experienced it during the opening of negotiations in Bali and during the WTO Ministerial Meeting in the summer of 2008. It is unfailingly positive that the emerging economies are included, given responsibility and responsibility. But we see that it becomes more demanding to reach international consensus. And important questions are raised about the formal status of a forum such as the G20 – and about the relationship between the G20 and the vast majority of non-participating countries, and the other formal institutions, not least within the UN system. It is positive, even for Norway, that the G20 showed its power of action in the face of the crisis. But the G20 is a self-appointed group. It has not been chosen or appointed; it has no statute or regulation. The challenges of the financial crisis have dominated the agenda since the first meeting of the Heads of State and Government of the G20 countries in November 2008. But we see signs that it will be in the G20 circuit the informal basis for formal decisions is laid for a wide range of other issues, from climate to trade and who knows which other foreign-related areas. This raises fundamental and political issues in the legitimacy of decisions. Over 160 of the world’s countries, including Norway and the Nordic countries, do not participate. Together with the other Nordic countries, Norway argues to make the G20 more representative. This could be done, for example, by: is made through an electoral model that does not need to significantly increase the number of participating countries but ensures their anchoring and representativeness. A Nordic voice belongs to the G20, given the Nordic international commitment, our role in international cooperation, our total weight in the world economy and our financial contribution to the crucial financial institutions. It is the emerging economies – especially China – that have experienced the fastest recovery and that now represent the growth engine of the world economy. The importance of China in most contexts is growing faster than most people could believe. An increase in China’s exports of more than 40 pst is now reported. the last year and has passed Germany as the world's largest exporter. China is probably passing Japan as the second largest economy in the world and can take over the United States as the largest in 15 years. China has landed with the highest CO2 emissions, not per. is the population, but as a country — and, at the same time, the world’s largest producer of energy from renewable sources. Today, there is hardly any area in which China is not already the world's largest or second largest actor. And it's an actor who signed up with a completely different self-esteem than just a few years ago. This has meaning in almost all areas of international relations. The growth China has experienced in the last 30 years is in the best interests of its vast population and deserves recognition. China’s growth has reduced unvalued poverty and stimulated important parts of the world’s current growth. Today, China’s growth is more than previously based on domestic demand and not as much on exports. It strengthens the country's ability to choose economic policy strategies without compromising too much to the outside world. Before, it was only the US economy that was big enough to lead such an independent line, but China too now has this ability. At the same time, we see that China is vulnerable, and even Chinese authorities are concerned about the basis of Chinese growth. To date, China has deliberately chosen to play a more withdrawn role in international policy. This is now changing, on the one hand through its own will to power and, on the other, through the weights of politics and the economy: it also has consequences in international policies to include a series of political priorities. China is exposed, China must take responsibility, China must show its cards. The relationship between the United States and China, or G2, as someone calls it, will be of great importance. The growth of China has, in a sense, gone at the expense of the United States. However, the relationship is complex and characterised by interdependence — and therefore by mutual vulnerability. In Africa, we meet an ambitious China, with energy agreements and major infrastructure and food production projects. We get living insights into other forms of foreign and developmental alliance construction. China is putting quite other leaders on the cooperation it initiates, among other things. by focusing much less on so-called internal affairs, such as respect for human rights. China also claims to be stronger in its neighbourhoods. Energy security and supply lines are increasingly important here too. Although China is the world’s fifth largest oil producer, oil consumption has been higher than domestic production since 1993, and it is now importing half of its consumption. For Norway, this implies meeting Chinese interests in common issues such as: maritime safety and new transport routes. We note China's strong growing commitment to Arctic affairs. We have strong professional environments that can develop new opportunities together with Chinese counterparts. And we're experiencing Chinese interest in our social model, not least the arrangement of our welfare systems. This provides a basis for cooperation. Norway may also become the first European country to have a trade agreement with China, and its conclusion is a priority work by the Government. In view of China’s growing importance in almost all areas, the Government launched a China strategy in 2007. China is increasing its activity against the Norwegian market, and Norway is also visible in China. The government has decided to take part in the World Expo 2010 in Shanghai, providing good opportunities for reputation in a wide range of sectors. Also in Norway’s relationship with India, contacts and activity levels have escalated, with strong trade growth since 2005. The main new feature is the width of the Norwegian exposure. Business was first, but now comes research environments, organisations, culture and civil society. On this background, the government launched its India strategy last fall, and as I had the joy of following up during my recent visit to India, there among others. security policy discussions were on the agenda, with broad and sound content. The government prioritizes building up knowledge of the Asian major acts and of the global changes in power conditions that have been reinforced by the financial crisis. As a result, we also strengthen our representation at the foreign stations in this region. We should also do this by upholding the respect of our most important universal principles, such as human rights and freedom of expression. The increasing influence of countries that place less emphasis on these principles gives us a double challenge. We're going to and will expand the links with them – and we're going to and will do it in a way that doesn't go along with universal principles and legislation. This is also the focus of our work as a member of the UN Human Rights Council, as we have explained and discussed in this Chamber. Let me then move on to the third area, evolution in Europe. Norway’s starting point is clear: Europe’s development, political and economic, is at the heart of our national interests. This is our most important export market. If demand falls, Norwegian enterprises and jobs are directly affected. The European countries of the Framework are political and economic, allied to Norway, Nordic neighbours and close partners. If Europe's voice is weakened, through the EU or otherwise, this is negative in most contexts for our impact as well. Infringes tests now on the European Union and, in particular, on euro-cooperation. The financial crisis and its management led to an even greater deficit and further problems for many of the European countries that had already experienced a large budget deficit. Two thirds of EU Member States are currently under so-called deficit procedures, i.e. their budget deficit and/or indebtedness are higher than the criteria laid down in the Maastricht Treaty. In 2009, the EU area had a fall in GDP of about 4 pst. Worst has happened in the Baltic States, but also countries that are far more important to the international economy, such as Germany, the United Kingdom and Italy, have suffered a decline more than the EU average. The financial crisis has put the EU and the single currency on the first truly difficult test. A single currency, together with continued national sovereignty in fiscal policy, places great demands on the coordination of economic policies, as set out in the criteria for economic and monetary union. In the euro area, the euro has given a stable and stable currency and a relatively low level of interest rates, which has made high investment and high consumption possible in many countries. At the same time, the competitiveness of deficit countries has deteriorated and debt obligations have increased. In comparison, if a US state comes into trouble, health and social security expenditure from the capital will continue to be provided. Some such common public sector does not have the EU. Thus, the pressures on some countries are particularly high when exchange rates are bound. Member States that have not complied with the debt criteria over time are now closely followed by the European Commission, the European Central Bank and the other Member States. This applies, inter alia, to: Greece, which has submitted measures to be implemented to stabilise the situation. The restrictions have led to massive protests and illustrate the short way from the financial crisis to social and potential political unrest. Spain, Portugal, Italy and Ireland also face major political challenges as a result of high or rising public debt. We must expect that meeting the crisis will take much of the attention of EU cooperation in the future. In Brussels and in the capitals, measures are being taken to stabilise the situation in the short term and to facilitate growth in the longer term. The EU will strengthen cooperation on the supervision of the financial sector, including: the establishment of a new body for macro-prudential financial stability monitoring. Under the EEA Agreement, Norway will be able to participate in the arrangements being developed. In parallel with the management of the financial crisis, EU countries are working to further develop a common foreign and security policy and the establishment of a common foreign service in line with the provisions of the Treaty of Lisbon. The start of this bet field has not been painless. We see the clear ripples between a desire for stronger coordination and a clearer common European voice on the one hand and a desire, particularly among the larger EU countries, to mark their own foreign policy course and action on the other. The sum is becoming a more unclear European profile and a weaker European impact. We saw it in the course of the Copenhagen climate negotiations, and we see it in areas such as the EU's relationship with the United States, China and Russia. Some analysts point out that what we can expect is a kind of new economic normal state in developed countries, especially in Europe, with lower growth and higher unemployment for many years to come. The sum of large budget deficits and the operation of high government debt weighs public finances, but this also hampers economic growth and adaptability at a certain level. Many countries have to cut in and tighten in. But at the same time, it is afraid that this medicine may become so tough that it in turn weakens the possibility of new growth. At the same time, we should not forget that Europe today is equipped with a well-developed political cooperation network, in contrast to, for example, one. The 1930s. This is in the hope that the European countries will converge and not turn against each other. And it has turned out in the past that it is in such times of crisis that the EU countries are taking joint steps in the future, as we have, among other things. The 1980s saw the emergence of internal market plans. In this overall Europe – of which Norway is part – solidarity and cohesion must be demonstrated. We will see our contribution through the so-called EEA funding schemes in such a light. We will make a significant contribution, with over 15 billion DKK – 3 billion a year – over five years to the new Member States, under a renewed and up-to-date agreement that we set out to achieve at the summit of 2009. The initiative covers several areas, from climate to green growth, culture and industrial development. As an example, let me show that Norway is now the largest international supporter of civil society in the new EU Member States. The government is emphasising the use of these mechanisms in our contact building against these countries, which are also our new allies in NATO. So I think it's important to take into account that in this sinister real economic terrain, where many of our closest countries of cooperation in Europe are making painful changes, the contrasts with Norway can be even greater. We've kept track of the economy, and we have low unemployment. With our large petroleum resources and our trade surplus, we are in a special position internationally. This also imposes a responsibility on us. In the first phase of the financial crisis, we have helped to reduce the impact of the financial crisis on vulnerable neighbouring countries, among others. by providing bilateral advice to Latvia and Iceland. We have contributed extra resources to the international financial institutions, and we have maintained the assistance of over 1 pst. of GNI. However, we need to be prepared to increase the pressure on Norway — as a surplus nation — both through requests for aid to countries in difficulty and through searchlights on how we invest our profits through the State pension fund abroad. This underlines the importance of long-termity and recognition in our international behaviour, where the management of our financial resources is not a political instrument, but is as fully perceived as part of the Norwegian ‘footprint’ internationally. It's an commitment to assist Iceland in its difficult situation. The government shares this exposure. Norway, together with the other Nordic countries, is the central lenders to Iceland in the context of the stabilisation process developed by the IMF together with the Icelandic authorities. A first part of the loan has been paid out. Following the referendum on Iceland on 6 March on the State guarantee for loans from the United Kingdom and the Netherlands in connection with the IceSave case, the three countries are preparing new negotiations. It is essential that the three countries reach agreement. The Icelandic Government has consistently stressed that it will comply with its international obligations and follow up the stabilisation programme with the IMF. As Norway sees it, this currently provides a basis for the continuation of the IMF programme. This is a vision we will continue to present to the other countries in our IMF circle. So to my fourth point: evolution in the United States. The fourth year was the worst for the U.S. economy of over 60 years, with a historically large fall in GDP in the United States, an increase in unemployment, a record budget deficit and a high level of public debt. As an illustration, it is highlighted that the United States, and the United Kingdom, have incurred a debt similar to that built up during World War II. The financial crisis and the recession, triggered by Bush's time, have created a massive political pressure against President Obama and his administration. The President faces formidable domestic challenges: ensuring continued economic growth, improving the labour market situation and reducing the large budget deficit. In addition, there will be the need to ensure health insurance for all and to limit the growth of high US health expenditure. Hardly anywhere, we see a clearer link between the political circumstances surrounding an economic crisis nationally and the conditions for an effective foreign policy. The Obama administration's efforts to get through the health reform have burdened political capital and pushed out a wide range of other issues in time in relation to the necessary treatment in Congress. This has an impact on the possibilities of getting through issues such as new climate legislation, a new WTO agreement or new historical disarmament agreements. The need to keep the attention to domestically combustible issues is reinforced by the congressional elections for the autumn. The result may be that policy objectives that require transformation and long-term — such as: in the energy sector – will be under pressure. International issues requiring power, time and attention in Congress will also be exposed, balanced against the immediate challenges that economic downturns have created. An example is international issues that need to be ratified by the Senate — such as the Treaty of Law on the Law of the Sea, the agreement on suspension and the ratification of the START Agreement. Furthermore, we see how the United States is burdened by the last decade's two major military commitments – in Afghanistan and in Iraq. This has cost both in human life, in dollars and in political profit compared to all the other commitments that the superpowers of the United States are in. This burden on the United States with effects on its relative international position should call our attention: it has global significance, but we must also follow this closely in relation to the importance we attach to good and present transatlantic relations. For Norway’s safety, these relationships remain of great importance. This underlines the importance of engaging the United States in our North Sea cooperation, and of building further relations with the United States in the development of Arctic cooperation and in all the other areas where we work complementaryly and complementaryly to the United States. Norway is working closely with the United States to contribute to the Middle East peace process. Here too, we see that a regional conflict will develop without the traditional power centers having enough weight or position to force a different course. This has occurred in the last few days' struggle over the new Israeli settlements in East Jerusalem. Norway is taking a great deal of distance from these plans. They're international law-enforcement. They're putting the peace process in jeopardy. And if they are implemented, they will physically close East Jerusalem away from a coming Palestinian state and thus make a realistic two-state solution impossible. In a time of crises that are shadowing the landscape – finance, environment, food prices – we need involvement in the Middle East, from the EU, the United States, the United Nations and other actors, and Norway will continue to contribute to this. Norway’s involvement and vote in the Middle East is clear and shall be relevant. A new meeting of the Palestinian donor country group, AHLC, chaired by Norway, is scheduled to take place in Madrid next month. It is now that AHLC is the only wider body in which Israel and Palestinians continue to meet formally. Several point out that it is actually the drive forward in the AHLC that is the area where progress is being made – in a picture of the Middle East that is otherwise marked by negative news. In other words, the United States is facing growing pressures. At the same time, we must bear in mind that while the dominance of the United States is weakening, the country will remain the strongest individual nation in the future for a long time. Militaryly, the United States remains the only power to operate globally. The United States continues to demonstrate on a regular basis the ability for technological breakthroughs and economic renewal. And we need American commitment to achieve results on most international issues – climate, disarmament, Afghanistan, the Middle East, relations with Russia, cooperation in NATO, strengthening the role of the UN, to mention something. But the point is this: we have to be prepared that the US focus will be more inwards, and towards Asia, in a different direction than Europe. We may also be at risk for the same reasons or as an effect that other major countries are increasingly concentrating on their own national economic problems rather than addressing global and regional challenges. This also concerns my fifth point: developments in Russia. Russia was severely affected by the financial crisis and today appears vulnerable. In 2009, Russia had a decline in GDP of 8 p.p., as opposed to the 10 previous years’ growth of more than 6 p.p. Direct foreign investment has fallen sharply. The large fall in the economy after the summer of 2009 has been flattened, but there is a widespread recognition – also expressed by President Medvedev – that the Russian economy needs to be fundamentally reformed and modernised if it is to avoid stagnation in the long term. We should particularly note that such modernisation will require closer integration with the Western economy. It is strongly energy- and commodity-dependent and characterised by one-sidedness in the economy. The fall in energy prices — oil and gas in particular — has made few countries feel as directly about the economy as Russia did. All of this helps to make Russia a major ‘unknown factor’ in the analyses of the impact of the financial crisis on international policy. A study shows that Russia is no. 120 out of 183 countries in terms of business-friendliness. Today, 130 Norwegian companies are established in Russia, 40 of which are registered in Murmansk. Improving the framework conditions in Russia is necessary to ensure that even more people find it attractive and secure to engage in the country. The Russian market offers great opportunities, as Norwegian companies have experienced, and cooperation on innovation, research and technology development is a priority for Norway. At the same time, significant risks remain, including political risks, to investments in Russia. It is Russia's responsibility to improve this, as the president of the country has pointed out. It is an objective of the Government to link Russia more strongly to international economic cooperation, including: through OECD and WTO membership. Bilaterally, we note that our relationship with Russia is consistently good. The main line of Norwegian policy towards Russia has been and is stability and long-term. We are seeing today the results of almost 20 years of conscious Norwegian commitment to trust building, project cooperation and development of the contact areas following the fall of the Soviet Union – especially in the north, where the environment and nuclear safety also have an important place. At the same time, we are aware of several worrying developments in today's Russia, including: with regard to human rights, freedom of expression and the conditions of a free civil society – the conditions that we regularly address in our discussions with Russian leaders. In other words, we have a broad and important agenda when the Russian President comes on a state visit to Norway during the spring. My sixth reasoning relates to the future of investment and the follow-up of the global common good. Examples of global common resources can include combating extreme poverty and hunger, global health measures, educational efforts, food security and broad efforts to combat climate change. We need to see in our eyes that increasing attention directed at the domestics towards painful mitigations can make it more difficult to finance solutions to global development tasks. The financial crisis has led to lower industrial output and, in isolation, to a reduction in climate emissions — in the short term — from several countries. However, the real economic and real policy consequences of the financial crisis can lead to a reduction in global climate challenges on the agenda of the peoples and politicians, and a deterioration in the technological growth needed to bring about cleaner transport, energy and industrial production. We need to be careful to draw the blatant conclusions on how the two crises – finance and climate – affect each other. But when this acute phase of the financial crisis is over, efforts to create a truly sustainable growth must be given new priority. This will have Norway’s full focus, precisely because climate change impacts may be more dramatic than the financial crisis, both in terms of power over natural resources, energy policies, distribution, migration and other factors. Towards the United Nations Climate Change Conference in Mexico in November/December Norway is working among others. with the negotiations on forest measures in developing countries and the establishment of a forest mechanism. Prime Minister Jens Stoltenberg sits in an advisory group appointed by the Secretary-General of the United Nations, where Gordon Brown and Meles Zenawi are co-chairmen. This group is working on financing commitments in Copenhagen Accord and, in particular, on long-term financing. The Prime Minister will also host a high-level forest meeting at the end of May this year. The aim of the meeting is to establish a global forest partnership that can underpin a common forest mechanism for the preservation of the tropical forests of the world. Furthermore, Norway is working towards Mexico with climate adaptation and with mechanisms and incentives for carbon capture and storage in the form of offensive influence work at political level towards other countries. In the short term, mitigation measures resulting from the financial crisis may result in countries cutting into climate finance and in defence and aid budgets. We now see signs that the struggle for resources is intensifying in international organisations. According to current OECD estimates, donor countries will pay 107 billion US dollars in development assistance in 2010 and not nearly 130 billion, as suggested in 2005 in the so-called Gleneagles promises of the G8 meeting. Here is Norway – unfortunately, we might say – in a special position by reaching 1 pst. of GNI in aid. The consequences can be serious for the world’s poor countries, where the lack of social security networks and rising food prices make job losses a struggle for life. Although a number of other factors such as trade and investment are more important than assistance to ensure growth and development, we must not underestimate the importance of aid for humanitarian work and international mobilisation for poverty reduction. Weakness in the will of rich countries to respond to poverty can increase global tensions between rich and poor countries. The global financial crisis has helped to widen the gap between poor and rich. Only in the last year has there been 100 million more hungry and undernourished, and about 64 million more people live below the limit of extreme poverty in 2010. This will affect the work of the Millennium Goals and, in particular, the fight against poverty and hunger. The UN Summit on the Millennium Goals in New York in September is an important opportunity to settle the situation and, where appropriate, adjust the course. At the autumn summit, Norway will also be particularly concerned about getting a new start on child and mealmaking. Preparations for this have started from the Norwegian side. Women can be hardest hit by the financial crisis, even though men are directly affected by the growing unemployment. However, women dominate the workforce of the textile and other export and tourism industries, and these are highly vulnerable sectors. Furthermore, there is reason to fear that the financial crisis is putting increasing pressure on the rights of workers and that it is further testing the fight for decent work. Norway will do its part to keep this matter on the agenda, including: in the ILO and the WTO. Illegal capital flows, often channelled through tax havens, also constitute a significant obstacle to economic and social growth in many developing countries. This has become clearer during the financial crisis. In countries such as the United States, the United Kingdom and Germany, and in the EU, the G20 and OECD, there is increasing attention to tax avoidance issues. A space has been created internationally to address the devastating consequences of such tax evasion and illegal capitalisation for developing countries. We will take advantage of this space, and this work is central to Norwegian foreign and development policy. In the final part of the presentation, let me mention some individual areas that will be at the centre of the time – areas where the financial crisis does not have a direct impact, but indirectly because the current international climate resulting from the crisis is significant. First about NATO. NATO is now developing a new strategic concept that will guide alliance policies for many years to come. Since the adoption of the previous concept in 1999, NATO has been extended from 16 to 28 members. New things dominate. Terrorism is high on the global security policy agenda. NATO is an actor in Afghanistan, far outside the North Atlantic region. One of the main tasks is to identify the security challenges that will characterise NATO over the next decade and how they will meet. In parallel with the work on a new strategic concept, proposals shall be made for major changes to the NATO command structure and other organisation. In this work, the Government prioritises four main issues. Firstly, the partnerships. Through them, NATO has helped to ensure stability and development in Europe. Following the enlargement of the alliance, there is a need to adapt NATO’s various partnerships to increase the yield for partner countries and NATO, precisely because NATO’s partner countries are different and have different interests in cooperation with the alliance. Secondly, Russia, NATO’s main partner country. Cooperation in the NATO-Russia Council has gone through waves. Norway places emphasis on building trust between NATO and Russia. We want to ensure agreement on an ambitious objective for practical cooperation and political dialogue with Russia. There will be areas where we disagree, but these should not prevent cooperation where we have common benefit. Thirdly, searchlights on NATO's cooperation with other international organisations, in particular the United Nations and the EU. Afghanistan has shown how necessary this is. The government is working to strengthen cooperation with other organisations both in the field and in the headquarters. Fourth, focus on NATO's core task. The government still wants to focus on NATO's fundamental core task, namely collective defence. Our initiative to raise awareness in NATO about both collective defence and the neighbourhoods of the alliance is not about a kind of ‘derailment’ from NATO’s operation in Afghanistan. Norway fully supports this effort. One of the objectives of increased focus on the territory and neighbourhoods of the Alliance is to strengthen NATO's position within and outside as a legitimate and credible security guarantor. This foundation is a prerequisite for NATO's operations outside the core area. Furthermore, NATO’s function and future cannot be measured unilaterally according to the evolution of the operation in Afghanistan. NATO, through ISAF participation, is doing an important job in Afghanistan, but this contribution is only one part of the complex effort needed to succeed. We cannot measure NATO's importance to us by how the overall effort in Afghanistan is developing mutually. For the Government, continued NATO membership is a pillar of security policy. I would also like to add, in the light of the debate this winter, that our focus on NATO's neighbourhoods – for our northern regions in question – is not a message that we consider our neighbourhoods to be more vulnerable or threatened than before. Our intention is to stress that NATO's core task remains unchanged, that collective defence is the core wall, that our allies should know about the changes that are happening to us, as we should know about the realities that are close to them, that the sum of this is about the indivisibility of security. We are in an important time for international disarmament – a field of government is a high priority. This is about security, development, the promotion of international legal order, humanitarian law and human rights. As a method, it is about inclusive negotiation processes, with partnerships between states, new groupings, civil society and also the affected. We are in an important time and in a time of hope. There's several reasons for that. The United States and Russia are close to a new START agreement on the interception of long-range nuclear weapons – the first real disarmament agreement for nuclear weapons in two decades if it is concluded. In May, the United Nations Conference of Supervisors for the Non-Proliferation Agreement (NPT) and NATO discusses issues related to the strategic concept of the alliance. And when the two countries Burkina Faso and Moldova handed over their ratifications to the United Nations Convention on Class Ammunition on 16 February of this year, we reached the 30 States necessary for this Convention to come into force from 1 August of this year. These are positive signs in a politically mined landscape. At the same time, we know that the challenges are great. Neither in the Conference of Supervisors, NATO nor in concrete negotiations has it been stated that Norway’s position is emerging. The world community has acute challenges related to Iran’s and North Korea’s nuclear programmes. They'd rather be bigger than smaller in the last year. The latest report on Iran from the International Atomic Energy Agency gives all cause for concern. Iran is responsible for proving that the concerns of the IAEA and the international community are groundless. If not, new Security Council sanctions will force. In the disarmament work, there are two considerations overseeing the Government: firstly, that we are able to use the new openings available for real progress in the various disarmament issues. Secondly, we are helping to adapt the international architecture of cooperation in the disarmament field to today's reality. This means that negotiations in closed rooms, with few participants, cannot be exclusive. Disarmament is a global issue. The negotiation processes must therefore include all relevant actors, both new state actors, civil society and the victims. The interest of ever-increasing new actors in the acquisition of nuclear weapons is clear. It happens in a security policy image that is more inconspicuous than during the Cold War. The fear that nuclear weapons will be used — not for deterrent purposes, but for terrorist purposes — is real. And the greater the spread, the greater the chance that they will be used. The objective of the non-proliferation agreement is to abolish nuclear weapons. The vision of a world without nuclear weapons was repeated by President Obama in his speech in Praha last April. This ambition is based on three pillars, and there are three base pillars of the NPT: non-proliferation disarmament peaceful use of nuclear technology It will be difficult to implement new non-proliferation measures if the will of nuclear power does not appear to be genuine. That is why it is so vital that the two nuclear weapons states of the United States and Russia should be able to enter into the current negotiations. And at the same time, if we're going to get real and permanent disarmament, we need a bottom-solid non-proliferation regime. In an age of increasing nuclear use, this is even more necessary. In parallel, we also need to promote what I would call an up-to-date view of the role of nuclear weapons. Although NATO is not a disarmament organisation, it is timely — about 20 years after 1989–90 — to discuss the deterrence doctrine of the alliance. It can only strengthen NATO as a credible defence organization that we openly raise the question of what gives us the greatest security today. Therefore, Norway, together with Belgium, Luxembourg, the Netherlands and Germany, took the initiative to engage in a debate at the NATO Foreign Minister’s meeting in Tallinn in a month’s time. Norway has been an international driver for the disarmament of conventional weapons with unacceptable humanitarian consequences. Several Norwegian parliamentarians have played an important role here. Through work on the Mines Convention and the Classammunition Convention, Norway has contributed to the setting of new humanitarian law standards. These conventions focus on the need to determine whether the effects of the use of weapons can or must be prohibited. Conventions have a major impact on the lives of many people. They have increased the commitment and respect for such international instruments and have helped to strengthen international humanitarian law and international legal order. Now we focus on implementation. The government has increased efforts to clear up explosives and help victims through humanitarian organisations and the United Nations system. This year we also have the presidency of the Mines Convention. Norway is a supporter of Laos, the world's hardest hit country, holding the first state party meeting of the Classammunition Convention in November. Every day, 2,000 people die as a result of armed violence, often with handguns. Millions are being orphaned, homeless, or deprived of their future. This is unacceptable from a humanitarian standpoint, a major obstacle to development, and it violates fundamental human rights. Norway places this challenge on the international agenda together with other countries, organisations and civil society. We will create a pressure for world society and states to take responsibility for preventing and reducing armed violence through measures and commitments. Together with UNDP leader Helen Clark invites Norway to an international conference in Oslo in April to provide the basis for concrete objectives for this work. The aim of preventing and reducing armed violence is also the starting point for the Government’s work on an international arms trade agreement. We want an agreement where such trade is subject to strict restrictions. States must be obliged to ensure that weapons are not used to violate human rights and humanitarian law. And the victims' right to help has to be recognised. We also have to assess the issue of the introduction of a retail declaration in all arms sales. The government is working on clarifying the possibility of introducing this as a norm in NATO. I mentioned our neighbourhoods, and finally I would like to mention some aspects of what has always been the Government's main strategic focus in foreign policy, namely the North. We feel that the northern regions are setting a new international agenda. Developmental trends in the north, challenges and opportunities are engaging neighbouring countries, but also far wider – similar to Asia. With our focus on presence, knowledge and activity, Norway will continue to be in a leading position to express cooperation and efforts in the north. This is true in the region we define as the northern regions – north of Norway, north of Europe. This also applies to the Arctic, which is now being opened up as a result of climate change and increasing interest in resources and transport routes. In view of this presentation's focus on international cooperation, I would like to point out some aspects of international, legal and political development in the north. Here we take note of several positive moves. Firstly, in line with the Norwegian view, there is a growing recognition that the Arctic is neither a popular nor a legal area. The neighbouring regions of the Arctic Ocean are integral parts of the nation states, and their rights and obligations extend to the sea and to the plaice, in the same way as in other seas. The International Convention on the Law of the Sea provides a solid basis for policy-making and no specific new treaty is required. This view wins the pass. In addition, there are a number of environmental agreements where implementation and enforcement are important. And even though the United States, as the only country in the Arctic, has not ratified the Convention on the Law of the Sea, the rules of the Treaty are followed in practical policy, and there are strong interests in turn in the United States that want ratification during President Obama's period in the White House. The five coastal states of the Arctic Ocean – Canada, Greenland/Denmark, Norway, Russia and the United States – will meet in Ottawa next week to continue discussions on issues related to our responsibilities in the north. Discussions contribute to transparency and to building a good framework for international cooperation in this region. The UN Convention on the Law of the Sea provides clear guidance on jurisdictional issues. One of them relates to the extent of the continental shelf. As the first Arctic State, Norway received last year’s final recommendation from the Continental Soccer Commission. It states that our base extends from the mainland to a point far north of Svalbard. This is an important clarification, and the bodies of Storting have been kept informed about this work. Secondly, the Arctic Council is the Central Arctic Cooperation Organisation. Norway wants the Arctic Council to reaffirm its role as a policy-making body for Arctic issues. In agreement with Denmark and Sweden, the Norwegian Arctic Presidency established a secretariat in Tromsø. It now serves the Danish and then the Swedish Presidency. We want to create a permanent secretariat, which would significantly strengthen the organisation, not least in the information field. But there has not now been an agreement to bring up new fixed observers to the Arctic Council. Countries like China and Japan, and the European Commission, are knocking. There shall be a clear difference between permanent members and observers, but Norway’s position is that actors such as these can contribute to strengthening the Arctic Council. Furthermore, Norway is in regular contact with China on the climate and environmental challenges in the Arctic, and both China and India are known to be established with research stations in the New Ålesund on Svalbard. Otherwise, let me underline the emphasis we attach on cooperation with the Nordic countries, as expressed in the follow-up to the Stoltenberg report, which has the full attention of the other four countries. Thirdly, with increased international focus on the north, we are working to ensure that the northern regions remain characterised by low voltage. Increased interest in the northern regions is not negative, threatening or necessarily conflict-making. We are to exercise sovereignty and authority in the north in a credible and predictable way, not in response to the experience of military threat from our old mental maps, but because increased activity requires more Norwegian presence, in the form of, among other things. enhanced oversight, enhanced search and rescue services and improved maritime surveillance systems. The defence has an important role to play in the protection of Norwegian interests in the north, also in areas that are primarily civil. This is why the Government strengthens our operational capacity by, among other things. new frigates and a coastguard with increased capacity. Several international forums in addition to Arctic Councils, such as Barents Cooperation and the Northern Dimension, play important roles. The International Maritime Organisation, the IMO, is also among them, and I was recently in London to discuss progress in the adoption of an internationally binding regulatory framework for Arctic shipping. Norway has played a leading role here. Fourthly, and finally, our desire to see the many political perspectives in the north — including those of foreign policy — makes it even clearer in the context that the Government aims to present a communication to the United Kingdom on the Northern Region policy during this period. In sum, we can see that the northern regions are one region we share with several other actors, allies and neighbours, and that none of the opportunities or challenges in the north can meet Norway alone. Similarly – but on a completely different scale – we see how the global financial crisis in the field of regional policy has an impact on foreign policy and international cooperation at large. Any problem in our world can quickly become everyone's problem. The place 'far away' is hardly any more in our electronic age, where people, goods, viruses, capital and statements are as well travelled. Furthermore, matters will dominate the attention. But we should also focus on the heavy driving forces that form the framework of foreign policy, because ultimately our security depends on the ability of states to meet these challenges together.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e460	The background to the Government’s legislative proposals is the coverage of VAT fraud in intra-EU carbon allowances trading. According to Europol, VAT fraud could have led to the loss of around EUR 5 billion by the Member States, i.e. significant amounts. The European Commission has made an urgent proposal to amend the VAT Directive. Several Member States, including Denmark, have followed up on their own actions. It is good, but the more countries that adopt countermeasures, the more likely it is that such fraud can be moved to Norway. The Government therefore proposes, as an urgent proposal, that the purchaser be responsible for the calculation and payment of VAT on domestic sales of climate allowances to traders and public undertakings, in order to mitigate VAT fraud. It is a proposal that the whole committee supports. I would like to thank the Committee for contributing to the rapid progress of this matter, and that we now have the legislative proposal finalised in the Grand Duchy before Easter. I refer you to the committee's unanimous recommendation to the Stortinget.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e497	The transport sector accounts for the largest share of Norwegian greenhouse gas emissions, with a total of 32 pst. In addition, emissions in this area have increased most in recent years. The Storting Climate Change Agreement sets targets for a decline in domestic climate emissions that requires shifting the increase in emissions in transport to a strong decline. Fuel charges will be one of the means of achieving this objective. The majority of the Committee – The Working Party, the Centre Party and the SV – point out that the fees need to stimulate more environmentally friendly behaviour by helping to reduce the total amount of car transport, while at the same time wanting a greater share of car transport to be done with environmentally friendly fuels. A third prerequisite for the charges is that all road users are required to pay the costs associated with the use of the road. The members of the Committee from the Right, the Christian People's Party and the Left point to the need for predictable fuel charging policies. They see a large potential for CO2 cut through major phase-in of biofuels. In addition to this, they shall be able to make their own views known. The members of the Committee from the Progress Party are not concerned with emissions reductions in their comments, but are primarily concerned with the need to reduce the total revenue from fuel taxes and other taxes on motor vehicles. The Progress Party believes that high fuel prices contribute to increased centralisation. They will probably explain this in more detail. The majority of the Committee is concerned about the need for a long-term approach to fuel taxes. That is why we believe that fundamental assessments should be made in the context of the State and National Budget. I attend such a review in connection with next year's budget. There will be no more predictability of a review already in the revised budget. SV would stress the importance of having two tanks in your head at the same time, namely to have charges that reduce the overall use of cars, while at the same time ensuring that more of the use of cars occurs with less polluting fuels. There are several instruments to achieve this objective. There has never been so much use of biofuels in Norway as today due to the marketing ban, which imposes at least 2,5 pst on oil companies. the fuel they sell shall be biofuel. The government has announced that this should be increased to 3.5 pst. in years and 5 pst. Next year. We have also agreed with oil companies to report on the climate benefit associated with increased use of biofuels. SV believes it is important to establish a long-term plan for further stepping up the biofuel share. SV agrees with the Progress Party that car charges should cover the total cost of car and car use in society. It also includes, of course, the environmental costs of car use, and the car is getting away too cheap today. We also agree with the Progress Party that Norway is a long-distance and widely populated country with large distances, and that this means that the car has come to stay. But I would like to point out that it is not on the outskirts that the car is the most driven, but in areas that I myself come from, that is, around medium-sized cities like Tønsberg, or in Akershus for that matter. In the SV’s view, there is a need for a tax regime that is both easy to understand and easy to follow up and that does not create tax gaps and too many exemptions. In this context, we find it interesting to look at the proposal submitted by the ZERO Environment Foundation. They point out that the CO2 tax on diesel and petrol in Norway is much lower than in Sweden and much lower than the price of the measures needed to reduce domestic greenhouse gas emissions. An exchange of CO2 tax and road tax in Norway towards Sweden’s level of charges will help to make non-fossil fuels more reasonable. At the same time, it can reduce the need for other specific rules. The right, Christian People's Party and the left refer to Climate Change, which points out that there is a great potential for emissions cuts. At the same time, climate change shows that total transport work needs to be reduced, and the report proposes a strong increase in the fuel price. We had a notice of a doubling of the gas prices. I don't think that's realistic. It must be prepared to have an increase in the fuel price, but it must be combined with a large-scale effort on the alternatives, namely public transport and rail. I hope that in the 2011 state budget we will be able to present a proposal for fuel taxes that will meet the majority comment and can agree on fuel taxes in the future.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e539	It is good to hear that SV agrees with the Progress Party that Norway is a long-distance country and spreads the population, but there also stops all consensus. One cannot talk about car charges without seeing all the charges associated with the car and car use in the context. The proposers have picked out one of the fees, the fuel tax, based on the climate problem. I agree with the proposers that we must have a predictable charging policy, which the debate on the biodiesel tax has shown to be complete that we do not have. I also agree with the rapporteurs that developments in the industrial and technological environments we have in Norway need to be facilitated. This, of course, is not done by introducing or increasing the levy for a type of fuel overnight, as the Government did in the biodiesel case in the budget for this year. Both manufacturers and users of this type of fuel had made large investments that became unprofitable overnight, without any kind of notice. Such a way of dealing with operators who wish to be environmentally aware causes any investors to miss this market, and users are reluctant to invest environmentally soundly because the regime of rules and charges changes overnight. This is unpredictability set in the system, which the Progress Party takes a distance from. If you want to see the whole picture of car policy, you have to look at the total car charges, the accidents, the pollution, the operation of the road network and the fact that the car is a good thing for society, especially in a country that is as scattered as Norway. Facilitating the industrial and technological development of fuels alone does not provide the basis for lower emissions, or rather, emissions from the Norwegian fleet are based not only on the types of fuels on the market but at least on the same rate of replacement in the fleet. Norway has an old car park in connection with the rest of Europe. It does not help that we have developed new types of fuel if we do not have a fleet that tackles this. To this end, the Progress Party shall promote the following proposals: ‘The government is asked to review the overall level of car and car charges in the revised 2010 national budget with a view to reducing them.’ This does not mean that we want to lower the car charge in the revised national budget, but we want to have the fact on the table before the 2011 budget is presented. As stated in the proposal, the Progress Party has an objective of reducing the fees. After reading the comments of the government parties, one could be inclined to believe that they agreed, because one of the comments says: ‘On a principle, all road users should pay a charge equivalent to the costs they incur to society in the form of accidents, queues, noise, emissions to air and road wear.’ I am asking you to note the word ‘corresponds’, because it is a fact that the drivers pay far more than the actual costs they incur to society. After all, there have also been several fiscal politicians from these parties who, in several debates, have stated that the car tax is a fiscal charge, i.e. a tax that intends to raise money for the Treasury. Now I have no faith that the government parties will take it on board – the desire to charge the population is too big for it. I have registered that to make the population act as the Government wants, the use of fees is an important tool. I am not so sure that this works entirely according to its purpose. If we look at countries where you have low or no taxes on the purchase of cars, a quick sign is that the sale of environmentally friendly cars is much higher than in Norway. In most European countries, CO2 emissions are by car lower than they are in Norway. This is, of course, because the car park is newer than in Norway, and thus the latest modern technology is used. I guess no country sells more hybrid cars, cars made for the use of E85 and biofuels, and electric cars than the United States, and there is no or very little charge on both cars and fuels. This shows that the population is able to make reasonable choices without the government having to force them to do so. It is in stark contrast to the strong confidence of the rapporteur in the point of charge. He may be able to explain why countries with little or no fees have lower emissions per year. car than what you have in Norway, where we have high taxes and the use of the bar. In addition to the different types of charges, the legislation for the approval of cars is rigid and slightly forward-looking. We have many examples that eco-friendly cars that are side-imported to Norway are not approved due to a complex regulatory framework. Among other things, many hybrid cars have been sent out of Norway and back to the country from which they were imported, because they had to pass complicated and expensive tests even if documentation could be obtained. There is therefore a basis for looking at the entire Norwegian charging policy and the approval legislation in the field of car policy. I take up our proposal and show that we will subsidiaryly support the proposal of the Right, the Christian People's Party and the Left.

This is the subject of a relatively broad consensus, but there is still no political guidance on the duration of such exemptions and incentives. No one deserves it. If the production of second-generation biofuels is to be competitive at an introductory stage, it depends on the exemption from the auto-diesel tax. If not, the Norwegian domestic market will disappear. The fees also do not take into account evening and night driving and the number of people inside the cars. The country must use the charging system to help ensure that the choice of environmentally friendly fuels is not unprofitable. There should therefore still be an exemption from the car diesel tax on all biodiesel, as this is still in an introduction phase. However, when the instrument is abolished, problems arise for those who have invested in new developments, who have invested in manufacturing plants, or who have bought a new – and more expensive – car in confidence that the subsidy scheme will continue. Such misinvestment involves socio-economic losses which the financial committee should be particularly interested in correcting. One way to avoid this is to inform in good time that the instruments coming will be phased out within a certain period, possibly when a market share has become so and so large. ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e607	The representative of Arve Kambe has taken up the proposal he referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e610	I would like to follow up with a few comments on what the rapporteur, Mr Egeland, said about the fundamental assessments. There are two aspects that are central to this matter. It is a comprehensive charging system, i.e. a charging system for all fuels, and that is predictability – predictability for all transport users and all fuel producers. Then we're talking about predictability in a time perspective of five to ten years in the future, because we know that there's going to be huge investment to generate fuels that are socially more useful than what they are today. Something good has come out of that debate, and it is that we have a system today that is not sufficiently comprehensive and predictable. It is the positive thing that has come out of the debate on fuel taxes recently. Furthermore, it is clear that the social costs for road users are high. On the part of the government parties, we are very concerned that the overall level of charges must be ensured in order to finance the Community's tasks. This view is widespread because alternative sources of income for the country are not particularly popular. The question then is, how are we going to set up the fuel charges for the future? What should be taken into account in the overall use of the road, and what should be considered in the case of carbon dioxide, which is the major challenge in reducing emissions? Furthermore, for a period of time, an incentive must be provided for a new type of fuel that will pay off both for the road user who uses it and for the person who produces it, so that it enters the market. It's classic economic thinking to change the behavior. What the government stands for is that a boat must have such a favourable phase-in system, and that over time the fuel charges turn towards a higher proportion of carbon dioxide in total. It is therefore possible to give a benefit to the fuel that does not generate net carbon dioxide emissions in the charging system in the future. There will be a central debate in the forthcoming budgetary negotiations and discussions on how to ensure completeness and predictability. The most interesting thing about what has been said so far is that neither the Advance Party nor the Right has any view of what they actually mean by the whole and predictability, other than that it is to do having the cheapest fuel possible and the least possible fees. It makes the government get a big plus, it does make it the government that has the control speed. ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e640	For the Left, there is no doubt that the climate challenges are one of the greatest challenges we face. The aim of the left is for Norway to take the decisive steps, during this ageing period, from being a fossil nation to becoming a renewable energy society. We want Norway to be in a position to develop and implement new technologies, create new environmental jobs and make changes in all areas of society that have climate implications. If we are to succeed in this, we must pursue a consistent policy that takes account of climate change in all areas. Despite the expressed willingness of almost all parties to reduce Norwegian climate emissions, the fasitium is the opposite. Total climate emissions are increasing almost every year, and they have increased most in the last ten years — from road traffic emissions. This is a development that cannot continue. Therefore, the left will have faster phase-in of zero-emission vehicles and another new environmentally-adapted technology in the transport sector. In reality, we're talking about biofuels, hydrogen and electricity. The problem is not that this is technology that is far ahead of time, but that this is currently not economically competitive, and that the abolition by the Government and government parties of the exemption from the car diesel tax for biodiesel only made hurt worse. Therefore, there is an absolute need for an overall review of the principles of charges for predictability both for producers and consumers, as proposed by the Right, Christian People's Party and the Left. The majority now announces that such a review should take place in the context of the presentation of the next year’s budget. That's good, but this is urgent. This should have been done already at the time of the presentation of the revised budget this spring, as proposed by the rapporteurs. Climate change 2020 states that it may be possible to achieve a reduction in Norwegian greenhouse gas emissions from the transport sector of a volume of 3-4,5 million tonnes of CO2 equivalent in 2020. If this is to succeed, it is obvious that there must be a comprehensive, predictable charging policy that stimulates environmentally friendly fuels rather than fossil fuels. The left considers that it is at least necessary to meet the objectives of Climate Change 2020 that are realistic for the transport sector. The left considers it possible to halve emissions from the car fleet by 2020. If we're going to succeed, we're going to have to have another set of instruments. With the Minister of Finance present, I allow myself to outline ten different measures that will be essential for success: change car charges to stimulate the use of low- and zero-emission vehicles such as hybrid cars, electric cars and fuel cell cars to set annual, progressively strict emission targets for new cars and adapt the charging system to ensure target achievement ensuring the placing on the market of at least 10 pst. biofuels and hydrogen by 2013 ensure that 2nd generation biofuels are commercially available by 2013 at the latest, prohibiting the resale of cars that can only rely on fossil energy, from 2015 extending the use of the existing zero-emission trolleybus technology contributes to the construction of hydrogen infrastructure, biofuels and electric cars, giving freedom to introduce several environmental zones in urban centres, where vehicles have to comply with strict, defined environmental requirements in order to introduce strict environmental requirements for vehicles in public tenders, taxi concessions and in driving allowances and business vehicle schemes that public enterprises generally purchase low- and zero-emission vehicles; These measures could bring us together where the Left will: to a zero-emission transport sector. In the meantime, we'll have to wait for another investigation from the Government. Investigations are fine enough, but I would most respectfully say that it will not be the Left against whether the new investigation is somewhat more exasperating and far-reaching than what has so far come from the Government, both in terms of the will and the ability to implement powerful measures to reduce climate emissions here at home.
