ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e1184	In danger of extending the debate unnecessarily, I would like to talk a little bit about what is the background to the legislation we have today, for it was the Council of State, among other things. Busy. In Equation-ABC it says: «The benefit in the form of welfare measures in employment is taxable income of the employee. Exemption from the tax obligation shall apply if the measure consists of a performance in kind and is considered to be reasonable welfare measures.’;» Further: ‘ Welfare measures within the meaning of the Tax Code are measures which: address the persons related to the enterprise/company and intend to increase the well-being and coherence of the workplace and related thereto, most often in the form of joint arrangements.’;» So this right over here is defined in the ABC. For example, it would be a reasonable welfare measure if the employer himself intervened or rented his own trim for the employees. Reasonable welfare measures do not include personal membership of a health studio or training centre paid by the employer. The consequence of current tax legislation is that enterprises can circumvent tax laws by purchasing a certain number of impersonal training cards that employees can use for training. It qualifies under the law. However, the impersonal card system does not work in practice, because it excludes those who need it the most, because of course the most eager ones take the cards. In addition, the scheme prevents the possibility of personal follow-up by training centres, which is essential for improving public health. The training tax also discriminates against large and small enterprises, as opposed to what the Council of State points out, because larger enterprises have far more training spaces in the workplace – including the Stortingen, so that we can enjoy it. Today’s legislation can also be considered to be particularly favourable to male workers, because non-taxable occupational sport measures are dominated by the male employees of the enterprises. So maybe today's system is a little bit old-fashioned. Once upon a time, there was human dominance in the workplaces around it. It is no longer to the same extent, and then it may be natural to also facilitate activities that women in particular want to take advantage of. There are relatively few women who are involved in corporate football and corporate bowling and other things, for which there are, of course, tax exemptions under the legislation, so maybe you should also look at this in that perspective. The system already exists in social-democratic Sweden – when it was introduced, Sweden was social-democratic. This is a tax exemption of up to NOK 6000 for training cards, i.e. twice as much as the Progress Party included in its 2007 alternative budget. It's been successful in Sweden, and there's no one over there talking about eroding the tax system and yelling and carrying for it. No, it works over there, and then you might be able to go to Sweden and see if there's anything we could copy from there. Sweden should be a leading country for social-democratic Norway in many contexts, so you can do it.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e1545	First of all, I want to make some reflections on the legal basis of the IceSave agreement between the Netherlands, the United Kingdom and Iceland. Iceland's accession to the EEA at the beginning of the 1990s meant inter alia: Icelandic banks were given the opportunity to compete equally with other banks in the rest of Europe. So that's not what the committee chairman suggests, that there were some irresponsible people who started off in Iceland that we could get the impression of. There is an agreement — the EEA Agreement — which also forms part of Norway, and Norwegian banks also expanded through cooperation with other countries. The difference is that Iceland is a very small country and has a very small economy. The consequence was that Iceland’s three major banks chose to expand their business abroad, because that was where the customer potential was greater. For the same reason, European banks did not choose to set up banking branches in Iceland because there was not enough customer base. Through expansion in other countries, the three Icelandic banks had mostly a turnover that was ten times higher than Iceland's gross domestic product. This growth occurred both while the banks were mainly state owned, and after the banks were privatised in the early 2000s. The collapse of the financial industry in Iceland raises many questions. Some of these are legally very complicated and are in many ways dissolved legal land. The obvious thing is, can a country be held financially liable if part of the private business does not assume financial obligations towards non-residents business itself is unable to comply? In other words, is it really that Iceland as a nation is legally and financially responsible for losses incurred by private Icelandic banks? The IceSave agreement is based on such a principle, and it is precisely this principle that many Icelanders believe is wrong, hence the great popular resistance to the IceSave agreement. So it's not – again – as the committee chairman tries to suggest, that Iceland is making up its own mind. Iceland has not entered into any commitments, there are three private banks that were previously publicly owned, that have entered into commitments, and that is where the legal dilemma is. The fact that the Storting today is discussing the situation related to Iceland is of great importance. It is quite natural that Norway, as a nation, represents our friends in Iceland. Such a clear opinion of the Committee confirms that Norway is willing to contribute – regardless of the discussion between Iceland, the United Kingdom and the Netherlands. For the Progress Party, it is essential that Norway be willing to listen — and act quickly — if Iceland asks for further assistance, in addition to that provided by the State guarantee last year. The Progress Party believes that Norway must be willing to go far to assist Icelanders. Norway, as a nation — with our financial muscles — will be forced to provide such assistance if Iceland needs additional support in the form of loans and so on. Iceland is in this context no unit, and that's Iceland's argument.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e1594	The international financial crisis was just the occasion that led to the crisis in Iceland. The real reason was a neoliberalist policy inspired by Reagan and Thatcher. Hopefully, Iceland’s experience will now be an example of warning for all. Nyliberalism and neoliberalism policies have clearly failed. Iceland’s economy was dominated by three major banks, Glitnir, Landsbanki and Kaupthing and 10–12 Icelandic financial businessmen who built extensive business empires over the last 10 years of the crisis. The banks and businessmen were woven tightly together through mutual loans and investments, which were called the Icelandic Brotherhood. A massive economic expansion was based on massive borrowing abroad. It went well as long as the credits were plentiful and the interest rate was low. With the collapse in the United States, the Icelandic pyramid economy also collapsed. It was designed to facilitate economic policy when, in 1995, a purely bourgeois government came to power in Iceland under Prime Minister David Oddsson of the Independence Party. Exemplified by Reagan and Thatcher, he implemented a pure liberalist economic policy with massive privatisations, cuts in the public sector and total liberalisation of business and banking. Financial capital gained power in Iceland and dictated policy, and with the new total freedom space began to build up what was called the ‘North Atlantic tiger economy’. The problem was just that the expansion was based on excessive and risky borrowing, and they built sky-high financial card houses that were extremely vulnerable. ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e1692	I refer you to the letter I sent to the Finance Committee on the matter, and that ever since the Icelandic financial sector collapsed in 2008, it has been the Government's line of leading international cooperation to support Iceland. No 221 for 2008-2009, where a treated guarantee for loans from Norway’s Bank to the Icelandic Sedlabanki, as it is called. ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e1890	First, I would like to say a few words about the rule of action as the next Monday, March 29, celebrates the nineth anniversary. That is the formal rule of action. We have had a rule of action before, but I am not going to get into that right now. The majority of the population then adhered to the guidelines for the use of oil money in the future. The main point was that one should phase oil revenues gradually into the economy, at the same time as the expected real return on State pension funds abroad, as it is called today. The Communication and the statement by the Financial Committee pointed out that the scope of action resulting from increased oil money use should be used in a way that also increases the growth power of the Norwegian economy. Infrastructure, research and tax reductions were mentioned in this context. There is no doubt that investment in infrastructure can, if rationalised, contribute to increasing the growth capacity of the economy. However, it is not that transport investment alone contributes to increasing the country’s income and well-being in the future. Many of the purposes we allocate money to over the state budget have the same effect. This applies, for example: expenditure on education, research, health care – even investments in kindergartens are important for the future workforce. This means that appropriations for transport and infrastructure must be assessed in the same way as appropriations for all other good purposes in the State budget. They also need to be assessed on the basis of their contribution to promoting the interests of society in the short and long term. Creating a government budget is to choose between different purposes. The size of the budget has stated, by means of its allocation rules, that there are some principles on the basis of which the state budget should be drawn up. This means that all the expenditure and revenues of the State are to be included in the State budget, so that both the accumulation and the government are given the best possible basis for making their judgements. It has been suggested from many quarters that a person should deviate from the so-called cash principle and that annual budgetary decisions prevent investments from being carried out with rational progress. That's not right. For, within the current budgetary system, the Storting may adopt major development projects by approving a cost framework for the project and empowering the Ministry to conclude contracts obliging the State beyond the first financial year. This possibility is also used in practice. To give an example – not in the road sector, however: annual budgetary decisions and cash principles did not stand in any way in the way that the opera house in Bjørvika could be built with rational progress. This is also true for investments in both rail and road. The criticism of the budgetary system is, as I see it, not a detection of a failure in the system, but really wants more money for certain purposes. It's fine, but the debate could become more transparent, in my view, if one could agree that this is about priority, about how much one wants to use, not the budgetary system itself. Through the National Transport Plan 2010–2019, the Government has taken action to protect some priority projects by financing them in its own posts. This applies to E6 west of Alta, E16 over File Mountain and new double track between Oslo and Ski. The aim of separating individual projects in separate posts is to increase the possibility for the State Road Administration and the Railway Agency to see the development of entire sections in the context, in order to ensure the most efficient development possible. At the same time, the maintenance of the infrastructure can be a well-profitable use of resources as well as initial investment. We have seen in recent years a necessary distortion of the transport budget towards maintenance. This will be continued in the transport plan. To say it with a flash in the eye: whoever has enough money in the bank doesn't have to buy it on payment. Because no matter how tempting a payment can be in an early stage, it always gets more expensive than it seems. What Mr Halleraker is really asking is whether I am willing to use alternative funding models to avoid cutbacks in adopted plans. I don't know if that's so, but it kind of looks like the Right wants to find ways to bypass the rule of action, one of which in other contexts is a very strong defender of. Norway does not really need to finance large-scale expansions of loans served by future government budget appropriations, and debt financing of large-scale transport projects does not provide us with any increased real economic space for action. Private debt financing can even help to increase the cost of transport projects, as such financing can often be more expensive than the country’s own financing cost, precisely because the Norwegian State is one of the most creditworthy. In St. prp. No 1 for 2006-2007, the Government discussed the question of whether the current budgetary policy should be implemented in such a way as to prioritise long-term investment in assets rather than other expenditure on the State budget. I wasn't in that government, but I've read about it. Following an overall assessment, the government found that it was not desirable to introduce such schemes. In this context, it was emphasised that many objectives financed by the State budget can help to increase the future well-being and productive capacity of the economy – as I mentioned earlier: expenditure on education, research, etc. The Government also concluded that investments in road and rail continued to be granted in accordance with the applicable budgetary principles and that, therefore, no own debt financing schemes should be set up. This is still the Government's position. It's good to be quoted on any of the things you've written before, but no one is surprised that I support the position of this government, right now. That's the way it is in a system where you've led a discussion and come up with a decision. Finally, a little bit about corporate organization. The company organisation of state activities is primarily appropriate for activities which generate revenues in a market and compete with other producers. There may also be other aspects of the nature of the establishment, such as: issues of finding an appropriate framework for national governance of different activities, which can justify the organisation of activities as separate legal entities, as it is called. However, the desire to finance government expenditure should not justify the organisation of State activities. Companies that raise loans to finance government expenditure with loans that have to be served later through government budget appropriations for many years to come are nothing more than trying to carry government expenditure and debt outside the State budget. This has been recorded with so-called OPS projects. It is also mentioned in St.meld. No 16 for 2008-2009 on the national transport plan, including: it appears that the OPS model has given faster project implementation but that it is not possible to demonstrate either higher or lower costs than in traditional implementation. Furthermore, as I've been about before, it says that gains can also be obtained through better contract strategies within today's system. It is concluded that OPS financing of road projects means that the State is financing expenditure on road projects and that the Government is therefore proposing not to open up more OPS projects now. The rule of action is an instrument for making the use of oil money more predictable. In this way, the rule of action also supports monetary policy and reduces the risk of an expected strengthening of the crown. But this assumes that the rule of action is credible. I believe that various forms of it can well call creative bookkeeping, do not increase the state's budgetary policy space, while undermining the rule of action. Important and correct choices of the use of government expenditure must be made through proper and important political priorities in the Government and, finally, in the Grand Duchy.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e1951	First, I would like to thank the interpellant for addressing an important topic that has been debated over several years, and which, despite the testing of different organisational and funding models, remains equally topical, namely how to organise and finance investments in the transport sector in order to achieve predictable, coherent and efficient deployment. Let me first stress that we in the Workers' Party are very pleased with the NTP decision that was taken in the Stortinget last June, which says that we will spend NOK 100 billion more on transportation over the next ten years, as the Prime Minister stressed in the media last week after speculations about cuts. So for the interpellation. Firstly, I believe that we need to have a broader approach to this challenge than the interpellant’s focus on alternative organisational and funding models and the issue of exemption or not from the annual government budgets. I believe that we also need to focus on the decision-making basis that governments receive for transport investment, namely the national transport plan. The aim of the plan is good and provides a comprehensive plan and a common decision-making basis for investments in the transport system in this country. Today’s NTP is a good step on the road in relation to previous individual plans, but still the plan and decisions are marked by the fact that we have four different agencies planning, building up and maintaining each of their modes of transport. Our challenge is to see the needs in context and to develop, operate and maintain a coherent transport system in the country, in the light of the objectives of transport policy, namely road safety, the environment and efficiency. These four floors must provide a basis for this in the common transport plan we have, to a greater extent than today. For example, in the case of organisation, you have a previous right. proposed to split the Railway Agency into two State limited liability companies, and the website of the Right says that they want to transform the Railway Agency into a separate railway company responsible for ownership, operation and management. I have no faith in such privatisation or organisation to contribute to the achievement of the objective of a comprehensive, coherent transport system. On the contrary, I believe that we must maintain the transport modes as management activities. What we can assess, however, is to merge some of these agencies into one common transport network and one overall transport planning secretariat, in order to get an even better grip on the whole. Prior to the adoption of the National Transport Plan 2010–2019 at Stortinget in June 2009, different forms of organisation and funding were tested and discussed. Under the Stoltenberg I Government, it was decided to initiate three OPS projects. These projects have now been completed and evaluated. I think there is a broad consensus that the way in which the OPS projects were organised and the predictability that was in the funding led to these investment projects being carried out in a shorter time than traditional transportation projects, thus showing a better outcome in the cost-benefit analysis for that particular point. It's worth taking it further. However, the evaluations showed that there were no lower costs related to this way of financing and organising the projects. The Government and the majority of the Stortinget therefore concluded in the context of the treatment of NTP that we would not proceed with more OPS projects now, but instead facilitate the adoption of several major transport projects with a predictable financing through project funding in the context of the State budget. Project funding is particularly important for railway projects currently financed only by the State budget through the annual appropriations and not, in addition, toll and loan financing as road projects through toll companies. The railway works have estimated that they could have built new double tracks between Skøyen and Asker considerably faster and with a saving of SEK 1.5 billion if the project had been adopted as one development project, with project funding for the entire project. As a result, the NTP 2010–2019 Government and the 2010 State Budget have prioritised project financing for three transport projects, which were also referred to by the Minister of Finance, two on the road and one on the rail. The way the Government has done this is to put these three projects on its own budget lines to ensure predictable funding, so that the Railway Agency and the Road Administration can organise and implement these projects coherently. The point for the Workers' Party by engaging in project financing is to have several major road and rail development projects carried out continuously and in a shorter time, while ensuring smaller improvements and investments in the transport network. We believe that this is well done through our own posts on the government budget for the major projects, and annual assessment and prioritisation of the smallest, as the Government has now stated. To these alternative forms of funding: I'm open to discussing anything that could get more out of each krona, but I just don't believe that the answer is necessarily to run with what the finance minister calls 'creative bookkeeping', because that's what unfortunately is. It has something to do with how many engineers, how many professionals, how many cubic meters of cement you get – while in the Progress Party I have the impression that you have no idea at all about it. It says that these alternative forms of funding should go almost outside the ordinary budgetary process, they should go in addition to everything else they do. Then I think we're going to experience one thing, and there have been several representatives around here: what's going to happen is that we're going to get a special level of interest, a dollar rate and costs are going to be even greater than we've seen, and then we're going to get a little less way and a path for money. On the red-green side, we have shown that we are able to prioritize road and rail in front of other good purposes. We have shown that we are able to prioritise individual projects in the budget that have predictable progress, but we are also able to prioritise it within a more normal economic framework. ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e2288	There is no point in debating how much greenhouse gas emissions Norway is going to cut at home and how much is going to cut out. That debate was decided on with the climate agreement. The debate should now focus on the measures to be taken to achieve the 20 pst cut targets. of Norway’s emissions at home and 10 pst. out by 2020 – while contributing to Norway’s carbon-neutral status in 2030. We agree on all of this. However, I am concerned about the pace of the Government and the results we have achieved so far. Emissions in Norway are rising, and they are likely to do so in the next few years. Government action in climate policy – as it is clearly expressed through the postponement of the climate communication – makes it difficult for us to see concrete actions at home before 2012 or 2013. It is entirely in line with the stance taken by the Government on the climate issue. There is a disappointing distance between word and action. We even see that the government rather consistently and actively chooses away the environmentally friendly solutions. It is reminiscent of the biodiesel car diesel tax, the high combustion tax, the low landfill tax and the unambitious energy standard in the new government building, to mention some examples, where most of them happen to be in the Ministry of Finance. It may be good to remind the Minister of Finance that our descendants will not judge us by our will to promise, but by our ability to deliver. The lack of effort and results at home makes it all the more important that the Government succeeds in the investment in international quota purchases. It is positive that the Government is using the market in the service of the environment. The market is known as an excellent server, provided that it is properly regulated. The climate crisis is due to a market failure which, in theory, can easily be corrected. It's too cheap to pollute and too expensive to be environmentally friendly. The solution is to make it more expensive to pollute and more profitable to be environmentally friendly. Quota trade may be an important contribution to this, but it requires a quota regime that corrects the major deficiencies of the current carbon market. If the market does not function properly, both the world’s climate and Norway’s economy will suffer. No one deserves it. Norway shall cover significant obligations through the putting into service of the allowance market. By 2020, one third of the commitments in the climate agreement shall be taken out of action. The effort to preserve the rain forest, which the Government was forced to accept in the negotiations on the climate agreement, is, of course, excluded from this calculation. The need for quotas, or carbon credits, will grow significantly by 2030, as Norway will be carbon-neutral. It is therefore essential that the Minister of Finance, at an early stage, contribute to the further development of the quota system and ensure that Norway’s significant quota purchases actually cut the world’s emissions and that it does so in the most cost-effective way possible. There are many challenges to both the European ETS and the UN ETS. Under the European ETS, expensive cuts in the ETS at home will result in Norway subsidising emissions in the same sector outside. It will not cut emissions, just move them elsewhere in Europe. If we are to carry out quota cuts in Norway, the quota market must be tightened up as much as we have cut, either by allocating fewer allowances or by cancelling excess allowances in line with emission reductions. The EU's quota system has the advantage that we know it works. This results in a reduction in emissions in Europe, and it gives the polluter an economic incentive to implement climate action that costs less than the allowance price. However, a sustained low-level quota price will not trigger measures as real monks. The challenge here is that Europe's businesses are free to choose to move emissions to countries with less restrictive environmental rules instead of taking the cost of cutting emissions at home. The carbon leakage then occurring may cause world emissions to increase in the large picture, even if they sink in Europe. No one deserves it. Therefore, it was important to land a global carbon market in Copenhagen in December, and a disappointment that this was not possible. The EU quota regime works. There is more doubt as to whether the United Nations' quota regime is working properly. It is essential that the purchases of allowances actually contribute to the reduction of emissions. It is difficult and bureaucratic to verify. There is a significant risk that large parts of the funds allocated to climate action will end up as fees and fees to lawyers, brokers, consultants and public offices. The class struggle claimed on 10 February this year that only 27 cents of each climate quota crown reach the environmental projects they are intended to – based on an international survey. If this is right, it's very serious. Norway has good advice, but the Minister of Finance is likely to be the first to agree that we cannot afford to waste public funds. Nor do we have time to lose in the fight against man-made climate change. If disappearance, inefficiency and administration eat 73 pst. climate action is the unsustainable use of taxpayers’ and community money, but it is also very serious because international trading of allowances is one of the pillars of the fight against climate change. The question now therefore needs to be what the Minister of Finance and the rest of the Government are doing in order to contribute to more cost-effective management of the quota grid, so that the measures we are putting in place actually provide a global cut in greenhouse gas emissions. It should be a priority task, as trading in allowances constitutes – let us say – the last remaining element of the government’s climate policy. Pigs and bureaucracy are not the only problems with the United Nations' quota trading system. One of the reasons why it is difficult to find good projects is likely that the green development mechanism imposes strict requirements on the allowances. These include: contribute to technology transfer between rich and poorer countries. This means that, for example: hydropower projects will usually only be in the poorest countries. The government’s project in China was, for example, Member of the United Nations. Hydropower is not a new technology in China. In addition, it must be demonstrated that the measure would not have been implemented by the beneficiary country irrespective of the additional funding provided by the sale of allowances and that the measure would thus effectively contribute to the reduction of emissions. Most profitable renewable projects will thus fall outside a country such as China. The country does not lack capital – on the contrary – and is likely to expand water, wind and solar power projects if there is an economy in them. Of course, in other countries this may differ. The best proof that it is demanding to meet the UN’s requirements for greenhouse gas emission projects under the Clean Development Mechanism is that the Government itself has managed to meet only 660,000 carbon credits from a total demand of 25 million carbon credits to be met by 2012 – according to the Class Battle. That means that the government has less than two years to honour the obligations of the Kyoto period, and I meet with the Council of State's statement on how he will manage to deliver on that promise. The strategy seems to be drawing on far more projects than is likely to be approved, in the hope that some of the projects will pass through the UN. If this is really the strategy, it's not surprising that the quota grid is inefficient and that it takes a long time to target it. It is tempting to add that of the 660,000 carbon credits granted to the Government in the last two years, the Class Battle reports that 522,000 originates from a forest project in New Zealand. There is still an opportunity to buy allowances in other non-EU countries — so-called joint implementation — but the impression has always been that the Government wanted to contribute to the cuts in developing countries through the green development mechanism, not in developed countries. That the project is a forest project is no more encouraging, as the Government is very restrictive about entering forest into our own climate accounts. The project does not contribute to development or technology transfer in developing countries, but it is at least an approved project. This illustrates the difficulties that the Minister of Finance will encounter in achieving objectives, but also the need to look at the criteria of the Green Development Mechanism. A recent development could be to open up to demonstration projects for the capture and storage of CO2. What does the Council of State think should be done to further develop the quota system? If the system is not improved, would it be possible, correct and secure to achieve the Government’s foreign policy through the purchase of allowances? What concrete action has the Minister of Finance taken to improve the system and to ensure that the crowns end up in climate action rather than in red tape and inefficiency? I meet the Council of State's response and hope for an informative debate.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e2360	On several occasions I have made contributions to the generation accounts in a more economic context. Such purchases are also an important measure of over-fulfilling the Kyoto Agreement, as we have set ourselves as objectives. We are confident that we will be able to achieve our goal of over-fulfilling Norway's Kyoto commitments by 10 pst. by 2012. The CDM system is not perfect, it is project-based and heavy-weight in many ways. But that is the best system we have today to contribute to emissions reductions in developing countries. It is the Ministry of the Environment that is responsible, inter alia, for: work on the green development mechanism. The Ministry of Finance has primarily a role as an operator in the quota market. So at this point I am therefore answering on behalf of the Minister for the Environment and Development. The Green Development Mechanism is on the map each time the parties to the Kyoto Protocol meet, which was last done in connection with the Copenhagen meeting last year. Under the direction of the Ministry of the Environment, Norway participates in the negotiations on the further development of this mechanism. Norway, like most other countries, has sought solutions to minimise costs without reducing the requirements to demonstrate the climate impact of individual projects. Over time, Norway has contributed significant resources in developing good institutions and good regulation, and we have also allocated resources to a governance representation in the green development mechanism. In the climate negotiations, Norway is working to develop other mechanisms that can replace, possibly complement, the green development mechanism for the post-2012 period. However, a transition to such mechanisms could take time, and it is uncertain how important it will be in the period up to 2020. As I said earlier in my post, the so-called CDM system is not perfect, but that's the best thing we have today. In the short term, the option to the CDM system is not a better system but is not to do anything to help reduce greenhouse gas emissions in developing countries. We think that is a bad alternative. Therefore, the Government will work to improve the system that is already there, so that we can achieve what is a common goal, namely a total reduction of greenhouse gases globally.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e2414	All the honours of the representative Astrup, who addresses an important theme and follows up work that was also taken by the Progress Party in the previous period. In the political debate, the Government has boasted that their approach to tight quota allocation is the same as an offensive climate policy. That's right – if a tight quota allocation is that the government doesn't allocate all of the allowances as one gets from the Kyoto system, but it's makin' them instead. And if you multiply a number of allowances, you're going to have fewer allowances in circulation, and then you're going to have less emissions than you would otherwise have. Then you can say that you have an offensive climate policy. On the other hand, what the government is adding to the tight quota allocation is that you should allocate fewer allowances to Norwegian industry than our competitors give to their industry. It is not offensive climate policy – it is simply to cut costs to industry and destroy their competitiveness. It should also be part of this debate. The government must take into account that the industry in Norway is the ones to develop new technologies to sell products that are more CO2 friendly than competitors. It is not helping industry by imposing a cost or an extra tax, which is actually being done. Even more contemptuous is it when one in addition says that one should over-comply with the Kyoto agreement and do so by purchasing cheap allowances through the green development mechanism. Then the government is a speculator of the worst class – they buy cheaply from the poor countries and sell expensively to the rich countries. That's probably the exact opposite of it one tries to get through all kinds of fair trade schemes, like SV and other 'hyper', but in quota policy it's anything but fair trade you're doing. Therefore, the Government – and in this debate the Council of State – should follow and adopt an approach to the allocation of quotas to Norwegian industry. The Progress Party travelled several times in this room in the previous period the idea – which also comes from the Norwegian industry – that Norwegian industry will receive more quota allocations if they are involved and undertake to do more R & D work at home, with the aim of cutting Norwegian emissions in the long term. This allows industry to have capital directly used to improve their competitiveness, rather than to fill the treasury with the Minister of Finance. In terms of the green development mechanism, we're also very positive about it. There is one way in which poor countries can get started with some projects that will improve their standard of living, which could reduce unwanted emissions locally, as well as cutting CO2 emissions from a global perspective. We see that the Prime Minister cynically uses this mechanism to transfer funds to the developing world, just to buy their favor to be involved in new climate negotiations. But – again – it is a good measure, given that it works. On the other hand, the examples that came from the representative Astrup show that there are many reasons to ask questions as to whether it works according to the intentions. The Progress Party will encourage the Government to bring Norwegian industry out more when making a CDM project, to ensure that new projects benefit the recipient country – but also to ensure that a take-off with Norwegian technology is helped to commercialise this. This will stimulate R & D work at home, although the domestic market is not there because one knows that the Norwegian government, the Norwegian big business, has a policy where they will have an outdoor market using Norwegian purchases of climate quotas. There are projects that can make a difference. In fact, for once in climate policy, the government can be a helper to the industry, and not one who only imposes costs on them and calls it an offensive climate policy. Thank you for a well-judged debate.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e2547	The NVE’s proposal for ‘Requirements for competence, etc. of civil engineering and territorial concessionaries’, the Competence Regulation, was submitted on 18 September 2008. The Ministry of Oil and Energy takes the further treatment of the regulation. The Energy Act authorises concessions to build and operate installations. This is interpreted by the NVE and the Ministry of Oil and Energy to ensure that only the concession holder is responsible for the operation of the installation in question. The main content of the proposal is requirements for its own competence, requirements for the exclusion of services, the safeguarding of the competence of external service providers and requirements for concessionaires to demonstrate their own competence and to provide information to authorities in the event of substantial changes. So what are the reasons why the NVE and the Ministry of Oil and Energy now consider it necessary to come up with the competence regulation? The main reason is probably what happened within the network companies in 2007. Then some large online companies, including Lyse and Hafslund, reorganized their businesses. Different models were used, but a similarity was sought to eliminate operational and maintenance tasks to other companies. This probably fell especially EL & IT Connected heavily to the chest. Here the said network operators would streamline their operations and increase the figures on the bottom line. This process has had to be carried out by other industries to survive. The fact that the broadcasters, which are essentially publicly owned and which are monopolists, are themselves in the process of improving efficiency, shows the seriousness of this industry. The network companies and the rest of the industry are facing large investments. Companies need to renew the old network and build a new network to link new renewable production and consumption. They must ensure and renew competence and technology. A total of SEK 150-200 billion will be invested in the power sector until 2020, of which half will relate to the network. That is and will be a great need for increased staffing. Rigid requirements similar to those set out in the NVE’s regulatory proposal will weaken the basis for the energy industry to address the investment challenges. The NVE’s regulatory proposal makes it even more demanding to provide sufficient human resources in Norway, and the industry needs to pick up staff from abroad to a greater extent than without the regulation. The industry believes that companies must have the freedom and flexibility needed to solve their societal tasks. In this context, any regulation of the transmission system operators should rather be made by imposing specific requirements on the quality of the services to be provided by the transmission system operators. Requirements and any sanctions would result in the most appropriate manner for companies to act. The areas in which the concessionaire is to be competent may also be required without imposing requirements on the size of his own staff and without requiring that an important part of his services be produced by his own staff. A survey carried out in 2009 — a project carried out by Xrgia, in which 42 companies covering 1.2 million customers have blacked out — shows that companies using competitive outsourcing are of better quality than those not. A rigid manning regulation provides impaired preparedness and poor delivery quality. The study shows that outsourced companies have fewer/shorter interruptions and higher efficiency scores. The NVE regulation will increase costs without increasing quality/reliability. The regulation will prevent local cooperation between concessionaries, for example: cooperation on operational central solutions. The operating centre is important for the recovery of errors. In several places distribution network companies cooperate on the operational central function with a regional company with both power generation and operation of network facilities. If the regulatory proposal is adopted, a number of companies will have to change their cooperation with the associated higher costs without any improvement in their preparedness or need to be exempted from the proposed requirements of competence. This regulation will be particularly demanding for small and medium-sized enterprises. These depend on cooperation. It's going to be very difficult to build up all the skills yourself. The selection of staff and organisation is a balance for each company. Individual companies are best placed to find local solutions. In other words, strong preparedness requires locally adapted solutions. The regulation of the NVE is an obstacle to this. We will have a fragmentation of large and competent professional environments and entrepreneurship environments, in particular this group which professionalises its operations in several areas of activity such as network production and district heating. The company Infratek plans to establish a national emergency response force. They can then mobilise an assembly strength of 500 to 1000 fitters in a short time. This is particularly important if major errors occur. If manning regulations are introduced and competence environments are split between network operators and contractors, the driving force behind and the ability to establish such a concept could disappear in a few years. In Norway today there are 140 network companies. Some companies are large, but there are many medium-sized and small companies down to companies with less than 1 000 subscribers. If the rules of competence are adopted in the present form, it will clearly go beyond the small network operators, and the number will be reduced. The regulation will require individual companies to have self-employed persons to cover most of the responsibilities. It shall ensure that the production, transformation, transmission, turnover, distribution and use of energy are carried out in a socially rational manner. The Regulation requires network operators to have the competence to perform most of the tasks. Small companies will normally need more than one employee with equivalent competence in each function, as proposed by the NVE. The network companies have very strong objections to the proposal for a regulation of competence. Opinions to the Regulation clearly indicate that the network companies want framework regulations and that the NVE should make clear delivery quality requirements as they do today. Organization and staffing are something that companies are constantly working to streamline their operations. If the competence requirement comes into force, the efficiency improvement and the outsourcing of tasks are already in progress. The industry claims that the number of network companies will be significantly reduced. It's amazing that the Center Party, which wants to fence around District Norway, can go in the breeze to introduce the new competence regulation. There are many examples of good forms of cooperation between small network companies that need to end with this new regulation. Sunnhordland Kraftlag’s central management centre is equipped to monitor and operate parts of the network of nine local network companies to which SKL’s regional network delivers. The cooperation with SKL varies, and works well. The regulation will put an end to this cooperation. Good forms of cooperation are also available today within Nord-Trøndelag Elektrietsverket, Bodø Energi Nett and Fredrikstad Energi. A new regulation will mean that cooperation will cease, and each network company will have to employ its own professional staff. This results in increased expenditure and often difficulties in obtaining the necessary staff. Competence within each area is limited and the professional environment may be weak. Except for the Ministry of Oil and Energy, the NVE and EL & IT Association, no one wants to receive the new regulation. It is difficult to see that the regulation has a different function than that there will be jobs mainly for the members of the EL & IT Association throughout the country. NVE says in the comments that it has not been given that efficiency increases with the outsourcing of services, as it is difficult to obtain good agreements. This claim conflicts with the general understanding of the outsourcing of services and is in line with the Government's reluctance to outsource and privatise services. The regulation gives NVE large control tasks, without meaning better and safer access to electricity for consumers. The new regulation is a clear input into the debate that the red-green government has against corporate transfer and privatisation. The regulation will affect public owned network companies, especially in the districts. Therefore, I hope that the Council of State decides that this regulation will not be promoted. Before a final decision is to be taken by the Council of State, the Stortinget should deal with such a comprehensive regulation.

Manning and competence requirements are not a new element in the regulation of the transmission system companies. Since its adoption almost 20 years ago, the Energy Act has required a concession to build and operate electrical installations. The preparatory work shows that the background to the concession obligation, inter alia: there is a public need to monitor the operation of such facilities in terms of responsibilities and maintenance. The conditional concession must therefore be imposed on the real operator of the installation. It is necessary to require the necessary competence and staffing to operate a network company properly. The key issue in the ongoing discussion of the competence regulation is the extent to which the concession holder can put an end to operational tasks and continue to be regarded as the real operator of the facility. It is necessary to make a concrete assessment of the individual changes in the organisation of the network company in order to determine whether the provisions have been complied with. Suggestions for Stortinget now I can't see that that's the basis for, based on the discussions and decisions taken in the Storinget earlier.

Road maintenance went down dramatically, and a huge resource was spent on supply systems, not least a control device, to ensure that contractors followed up their functional contracts. I believe that there is a strong connection between holding responsibility for the day-to-day operation and actually operating it. If a set of operations is used, a set of operations also gives a good part of the knowledge and competence that you depend on in your own organisation. This makes one vulnerable, and one is given to others and the services they can offer at any time – just the experiences of a home from the Railway Agency and the State Road Administration. Purchasing competence is also a source of confusion. This poses a number of new challenges and questions. If the operator delivers a bad operation, does one then have the competence to assess the quality of a buyer? If the operator goes bankrupt or otherwise ceases to exist, one depends on others in order to be able to operate the service one actually depends on operating. This is an example of concrete events that can occur by outsourcing one service to others. One makes himself very vulnerable. Energy supply is too important to be the subject of short-term economic speculation. For this reason, I believe that the operation of the energy installations should be the responsibility of the owner as far as it is appropriate. Of course, as the Council of State also did, there must be a reasonable balance with this. But the main principle that owners should also operate is a principle that I believe the Labour Party will at least protect about in the future as well.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e2762	Unfortunately, this winter we have again experienced a situation of high electricity prices – extremely high in some parts of the country, especially in central Norway. Anyway, this winter and these high power prices show that we are vulnerable to a number of coincident events. partly because such a high proportion of our electricity production is caused by hydropower, and this requires, as is well known, a certain amount of rainfall, partly because the transmission system does not function optimally, partly because we are the net importer of electricity for some periods, and thus depending on other countries producing a surplus. And – what makes the situation of winter difficult to accept is that we have experienced the same thing before, namely in 2003. Thus, it is relevant to ask what we have learned from the 2003 price crisis, and what has happened in the meantime to ensure that people and businesses in central Norway would not experience this type of price level as they have done in winter. This could contribute to a lower price level, and it could help people and businesses who now have to pay high electricity bills, at least knew that the authorities were doing what was possible by short-term action to improve their situation. We need to make our own power balance more robust, and we need not least to build grid capacity so that the country does not have to be divided into different price ranges with such huge price differences. Unfortunately, the situation is such that it takes a long time to build new electricity transmission systems. It is planned for approx. 100 TWh new energy production, 30 to 40 TWh of which have been applied for or granted a concession. These plans include the development of wind power, district heating, small-scale power and hydropower. The replacement of the building stock is a relatively late process, but we are likely to have significant changes in demand for electricity gradually. We have to expect that electric cars will gradually take over large parts of the car market, and this will also lead to an increase in electricity demand. NOK 7 billion for the promotion of renewable energy through Enova. We have set up an energy fund that is now up to 25 billion kroner, where the return goes exclusively to Enova's work on renewable energy and energy efficiency improvement. We have strengthened the NVE considerably, precisely in view of the development of renewable energy. As a result, the concession treatment capacity of the NVE is roughly doubled compared to 2005. The Government has concluded an intention agreement with Sweden for the establishment of a green certificate market from January 2012. We have introduced energy labelling of buildings, which will be mandatory from 1 July this year. We have just established a maritime energy law that will be an important framework for the future development of renewable energy at sea. This is a project that would hardly have been possible without co-financing from the public through Enova. In June 2009, Enova had its largest wind power allocation ever, with DKK 1.1 billion distributed among four projects. These are just a few examples of the pace shift that took place after 2005 in our nation's energy policy in the field of renewables. In addition to the development of new energy, the Government has also made a major effort to improve energy efficiency, especially in the field of industry. It is a major potential for energy efficiency improvement in industry. From 2006 to 2008, Enova has been involved in some 100 industrial projects and is expected to save around 200 people. 2.1 TWh per years through this work. By ensuring the clean power of the industry, we are helping to secure jobs, while at the same time taking into account the climate. Securing the industry energy is important for the Workers' Party and the Government. Therefore, support for Finnfjord melting plants through Enova is incredibly important. In recent months we have also shown that we need a more robust power supply in our own country. A cold and dry winter combined with closed nuclear power stations has contributed to a tighter energy market here at home. In addition to domestic bottlenecks in the network, this has sometimes led to large regional differences in electricity prices. In order to improve this in the future, we need both better grids and not least more power. The Labour Party and the Government will in the future be the guarantor of an even more offensive energy policy, which ensures not least jobs and provides people with security of energy supply in their everyday lives – and that is the most important thing.

It provides investment in the districts for about $80 billion. It will have a significant impact on several thousand landowners and rightholders, as well as on the ring-fence of suppliers and other businesses. Small scale hydropower generation has a number of positive features in addition to strengthening the power supply. It is a renewable energy source that does not emit greenhouse gases, and it can create industrial development and new optimism in the districts. For landowners in the districts, income from small-scale power plants may give loved ones additional income. This is something that should excite an energy minister from the Central Party. I am therefore very surprised that the Council of State and the Government did not help to facilitate the situation of landowners in the districts who want to build small power plants, but instead have chosen to strain bone hooks on some of them. In the proposal for a transitional renewable energy scheme submitted by the Government, the Centre Party and the other two red-green parties have broken their previous commitment to include small power plants built after 2004 in the forthcoming certification market. Only the smallest power stations, with less than 1 MW of installed power, and small power stations built after 7 September 2009 shall be included. All others who have built small-scale power plants in the belief that they would be included in this support scheme, are now using the box. Of the 185 small power plants that have been built since 2004, only 35 are allowed to enter a green certificate market with the Government's scheme. For those who have invested in small power, in the belief that they would be included in the support scheme, this is very serious. These are landowners in the districts who have made large investments and made large loans, in the belief that politicians would stand by their promises. They are now in danger of going bankrupt and having to leave the yard. Unfortunately, this is not the only example of how the Government puts obstacles in the way of those who want to bet on small power plants. Before they have seen the earth's spade and started to build the power plant, small-power owners have to wait for years in line with Norway's watercourse and energy directorate in order to obtain a concession. Today, 550 power plants in application queues at Norway's watercourse and energy directorate are standing to get permission to be built. 100 applications are processed every year. This means that builders have to be prepared to wait five years before they can start building. Bringing this queue down will be an important measure to improve security of supply, as the representative Meling addressed in his interview. Here the Government has a lot to go on. Another measure that the State Council should look at in order to increase the output of small power plants is the entry point for the base rate tax. Today, if you build a power plant with installed power over 5500 kVA, you will have to pay an additional base rate of 30 pst. for all production, but if you build a power plant below this limit, you skip the basic interest tax completely. One should therefore consider whether it is possible to make this transition more slippery, possibly raising the entry point, so as to avoid a fiscal adjustment of small power plants just below 5500 kVA. One should in any case avoid lowering the limit further, as today's government tried a few years ago. Facilitating the expansion of small-scale power plants in the districts may be an important part of the solution to the challenges posed by the representative Meling. But it requires a completely different attitude for those who want to bet on such expansion than what we have seen so far from the Council of State and from the Red-Green Government.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e3008	The starting point for the undersigned, and I suppose most people in the debate, is that one should use our energy resources as rationally and efficiently as possible. It makes sense as a society, and it makes sense for the individual – given that it is done in the right way. For example, sometimes a few impressions that one in the energy debate, in terms of energy efficiency improvement, is going to use a crown to save ten ears. But done correctly in many ways it is a gold mine to cut our energy use, for one can maintain production in industry, one can maintain business, in the public sector, one can have comfort and prosperity in the home, even if a user uses less energy — if one uses it smarter. It is a great potential for energy efficiency improvement in our country. About 40 pst. of our stationary energy use is in construction, corresponding to approx. 80 TWh. And there are studies recently that show that the potential for energy efficiency improvement is up to 12 TWh. However, Norway does not have its own energy efficiency improvement targets, although we have some instruments through Enova. I'll come back to that. In order to improve energy efficiency, there can be a quiet requirement for new construction. It is a comprehensive, or long-haul process if you want to get to the goal, by replacing the building mass only by approx. 1 pst. This year. But it's not wrong to be long-term for that reason. But one can also think of stimulation by suffocation. This is also in line with what President Obama did in the United States in January 2009. I mention it, because part of the red-green side of this hall is very inspired by everything he does. Alternatively, schemes such as white certificates or the like may be solved. This debate should not be a review of the various measures, but more a discussion that targets for energy efficiency improvement often conflict with individual energy conversion objectives. What is important on the part of the Progress Party is that we have consumer interest in the centre here – not just the desire of individual industries to increase their market share. Because that's how it can work for some people when one sees what technology requirements come from the Government in the form of regulations. We believe from the Progress Party that a few to the best possible energy use must allow a variety of solutions, depending on the size of the dwelling, where it is, how old it is, what use it has. The only exception is the power consumption management system, which can be supported by almost everyone. But the rest of Enova's measures against normal households are based on measures that do not deal with electricity. And if you're going to isolate your dwelling, there's nothing to get through these arrangements. That, I think, means that one really gets less use of the resources of society that Enova has, because the use of tools is getting narrower than it could be in relation to people's needs. I would also mention that the aid granted per. kWh for energy saving is much lower than the aid granted by the same authorities to trigger a new power generation. It is also a paradox, but it is not what this debate should be about either. There are some paradoxes in this debate about energy saving versus energy conversion, where it seems that energy conversion targets to satisfy some industries are more important than the need for overall energy saving. There's someone coming, for example. when you start talking about district heating versus passive housing. In the climate agreement, all the parties that were involved – who were allowed to participate in it – stressed that passive houses are the future. At the same time, we know that in a very large number of municipalities, there is a huge commitment to district heating, because one sees it as being a political national goal. And the conflict comes when one in areas where one wants to build passive houses, is required to install district heating. It's the very few builders who think there's any sense in it, and then they choose one. And usually it turns out that they choose the passive house standard, because district heating is something they need, passive house is something they can have. That debate is often marked by the fact that local politicians say, but we must have district heating measures, because that is what national politicians say, while national politicians, and then the government, as I refer to here, says, but it is not a requirement, it is a call. But the call is perceived as so strong that it takes priority. It is rarely appropriate to retrofit the waterborne heat in existing dwellings. This means that the extent of district heating can be quite limited in some places where you want to impose it. One says that district heating has a utility because one often uses heat that would otherwise go to play. And right there, I fully agree that district heating has an effect. But we see in some areas that it's not necessarily just waste incineration that fills up the district heating plants, there's probably so much combustion of bioresources, and not least the combustion of fossil resources. So right there, I'm going to challenge the Council of States to present a CO2 account for Norwegian district heating production, so that one actually sees that this CO2 is as good as it wants to replace. Those numbers have been hard to get, unfortunately. As far as the energy labelling system is concerned, it is a basically good and sensible scheme to stimulate the efficient use of energy, and we thought it would be independent of which sources of heat a user uses. One shall make consumers aware at the moment of purchase of the kind of energy consumption the building they want to buy has. But here there is also a conflict between different energy sources, where electricity came out well, while bioheat came out relatively poorly, and the scheme was then stopped. Lookups in Today's Business give some explanations as to why, and I refer to the notice on 15 December 2009. With the poor output of bioenergy producers, there was a lot of protest, and one had to find a system that makes bio-heating come out better in this account. Heat pumps come out best, pellets come out poorly, even with the electric panel furnace coming out better than pellets. Based on an energy efficiency problem, I see no problem in it. But when the goal is energy conversion, of course, it becomes very problematic. Then you have to be open and honest about what the energy label scheme really is about. It's also a little bit of the attention you're trying to travel in this debate. I do not feel that the EU's intention was to push electricity out, but to increase efficiency in the use of energy in general. The last point I'm going to get into is the Technical Regulation for the Law on Planning and Building, which is now on a new round in the consultation system and the Government wants to tighten up. I fully agree here, too, that one should have a rational approach to the use of energy in buildings. But the new requirements with which one is now out there do not seem to be at all caring for the interests of the builders or consumers, but only focus on one's own desire to prevent the use of electricity in buildings. It appears almost as a separate goal, despite the fact that the NVE says that the current from the wind and water is very environmentally friendly, despite the fact that one in all contexts when discussing electric cars, talks about a user's clean power. However, precisely when discussing technical regulations, it is estimated that CO2 emissions from the flow in Norway are 350 g/kWh. So all of a sudden, a CO2 emission element appears in Norwegian power when you use something Government doesn't want you to do. But when you use it in any of the governments, it's zero. A selective approach to the use of facts is now important. Today, technical regulations state that 40 pst. of energy needs should be renewable – i.e. beyond electricity – but there is an exceptional rule that says that you will be exempted from this requirement if you can demonstrate that it is more rational economically to use electricity. The new technical regulations tighten this up by saying that building over 500 m2 should have 60 or 80 pst. renewable energy use, and you also remove the rule of exception. So you can't count on electricity being more profitable – you can do it, but it won't mean anything. And then you start imposing regulations and regulations that make it very little rational in relation to the use of the best tools for builders. This is especially the point of appeal when, through regulations, you change the entire energy supply and no longer focus on the economic rationality of the builder, but just say that political we do not want to use electricity in new buildings, and that is why we change the regulation, despite the fact that § 10-3 – that is, the point before – suggests that the energy demand in new buildings should be reduced. So at the same time as you're going to reduce, you're going to put in expensive technical solutions like you didn't reduce energy consumption. There's a conflict between energy efficiency and energy conversion that makes society not using its resources rationally, and I hope that the Council of State will sort out a few points.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e3057	Two important elements of energy conversion are: reducing energy use to increase the use of waterborne heat based on renewable energy sources, waste heat and heat pumps; Energy conversion thus includes measures both for more efficient energy use and for environmentally friendly heat solutions. When the concept of energy conversion was introduced, it was justified in terms of environmental and security of supply in energy supply. These are also important considerations in energy policy. This winter we have had these challenges clearly demonstrated, and that has also been reflected in the issues that we have debated today. Less dependence on electricity for heating provides safer private finances in households and more stable business frameworks. It is also an advantage for the power system. I believe it is important to take the environmental challenges in the energy sector seriously. It is important to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. It is also important to limit the use of electricity, so that we trigger less need for power generation and power lines, and can use electricity for purposes other than heating, where it replaces, for example. fossil energy use. The government is committed to improving the energy efficiency of the building stock. Building regulations from 2007 significantly increased energy requirements and the Government will consider introducing passive house requirements by 2020. Enova processes the market to enable contractors to deliver construction with ever more stringent energy requirements. I believe this work is important. The answer to the question from the representative Solvik-Olsen is therefore: Yes, we are going to bring down the energy use in our building. That's the overall goal. And point two: the remaining heat demand shall be covered by environmentally friendly heat solutions. The government is committed to increasing the use of waterborne heat based on renewable energy, waste heat and heat pumps in buildings. So far, this investment has contributed to a great deal of activity in the district heating industry. However, the development of district heating is not necessarily a comprehensive solution. Local heating centres are a good alternative in many places. Also for single dwellings there are good stand-alone heat solutions based on energy sources other than electricity and oil. I believe that the interpellant believes that there is a contrast between investing in energy efficiency improvement and exploiting environmentally friendly heat resources. I know there's a debate going on around this, but I think the debate going on in this area needs to be nuanced. About 75 TWh of energy is used in our buildings each year. It's a long-term job to bring down the consumption. Major measures need to be implemented primarily in the context of main rehabilitation and new construction, otherwise costs become unreasonable. In 2030, about half of the building mass may still be existing buildings that have not undergone a main rehabilitation. We don't have time to wait for all of these buildings to be renovated. According to the Low Energy Commission, it is estimated that 0.6 pst. the housing stock is refurbished each year. It corresponds to an average lifetime of 167 years for dwellings in Norway. Corresponding figures for professional buildings are a rehabilitation rate of 1,2 pst. and an average lifetime of 83 years. The energy demand in these buildings will therefore remain at a relatively high level, mainly using electricity and some oil. The possibilities for converting to other energy solutions are significant. We have heat resources that can be used locally, with low environmental costs. I think we should use these resources to heat up our buildings. In passive housing, the need for energy input is minimal, writing the interpellant in its interpellation. Enova has supported the construction of over 40 low energy houses and passive houses, and we expect to gain a lot of useful experience from these projects. We see good examples of souls in passive houses that all or part of the heat demand is covered by energy carriers other than electricity and oil. Renewable solutions are used and, in particular, hot water needs are met since the need for hot tap water will be the same as in other buildings. Calculations show that the need for hot tap water in a large single dwelling with passive house standard accounts for about two thirds of the heating demand. In an exclusive dwelling after today's energy requirements, hot tap water stands for a little above a third party that the heating demand. Furthermore, passive house is dense house, with energy-efficient windows. It allows for the use of simplified, and more reasonable, water-borne heating systems adapted to the low heat demand. We see a development in this area that is exciting. We also need to remember that many of the passive houses are large professional buildings and multi-dwelling houses, where the overall need for heat will be significant, and it could mean something for profitability and for the climate to use water-borne solutions. In future energy-efficient buildings too, there will be a need for heat. I don't imagine that it should only be covered with electricity. Environmentally friendly heat resources will play an important role. It's about security of supply. It's about the environment. We will contribute to a good solution in this area by supporting the development of new solutions such as: the modelling projects. The most important job we're facing is to reduce the need for energy in our building. It's a long-term project. At the same time, we must ensure that the heat demand that the building stock will have for a long time comes from environmentally friendly heat solutions. That's this approach I want to base my further work on in this area.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-23.ana.ud774e3144	The most environmentally friendly energy is known as the one that is not used. Therefore, it is disappointing that Norway is the only country in Europe that lacks clear targets for energy efficiency improvement. A study from SINTEF shows that it is possible to save 12 TWh in construction by 2020. It corresponds to the annual energy consumption of 600 000 dwellings. As the Right sees it, there are three things the Government should do to trigger the energy efficiency potential. First of all, the state must go ahead. Requirements should be introduced for new public buildings to have the character A or B of the energy labelling scheme. Thus, it will be impossible to do what the Government is now doing, namely to build a government building with a mediocre energy standard. Secondly, the Government should strengthen the requirements for the supply of energy in technical regulations for all new buildings and major renovations. Thirdly, the Government should introduce a tax-deductible scheme for an investment in private homes. Private households account for 68 pst. of energy consumption in the building mass of Norway. Private individuals need simple but effective incentives to invest in an increase in owner-occupiers’ housing. Interpellant asks if the Government's investment in energy conversion is at the expense of the investment in energy efficiency improvement. That's a good question. It's essential that we use energy more efficiently, no matter what source it comes from. Energy conversion is positive where it replaces polluting sources of heating with environmentally friendly alternatives. On the other hand, it is not an objective – and directly contrary to the energy labelling system adopted by the Stortinget – to favour bioenergy rather than electricity on a general basis, as the Council of State called for in today's industry before Christmas. The main purpose of the energy labelling scheme is to indicate the energy efficiency of the building, irrespective of the source used for heating. If the intention of the Council of State with the postponement of the energy label scheme is to change the scheme so that bioenergy becomes better, the scheme will help less to inform and more to confuse buyers and sellers of buildings and dwellings. The entire energy labelling system risks becoming completely worthless. As if that were not enough, the Ministry of Local and Regional Affairs has sent out proposals for consultation on new energy supply requirements in technical regulations, as the interpellant also had, with a minimum of 80 pst. of the net heat demand of buildings 500 square metres or greater shall be covered by energy supply other than direct electricity or fossil fuels at the end-user. For buildings less than 500 square metres, a minimum of 40 pst. net heat needs are met by energy supplies other than direct electricity or fossil fuels. In a country about 100 pst. of the electricity supply is covered by renewable hydropower, the Ministry of Local and Regional Affairs chooses to equate electricity with fossil fuels. This is proposed without any costs or consequences being investigated. A change in technical regulation that could significantly increase costs for Norwegian consumers is presented almost on the basis of pure vision, based on government policies. The proposal for a change to technical regulations does not strengthen the requirement for energy efficiency and the need for delivered energy for construction. The audit is a strategy for increasing energy conversion, away from fossil fuels and direct electricity – despite the fact that electric heating systems consistently lead to lower energy consumption and higher efficiency than alternative heating systems. The size target of reducing energy consumption and greenhouse gas emissions could be achieved by strengthening energy requirements based on the need for energy delivered by the building. The regulation should be technology neutral. Changes in energy use requirements in buildings must be evidence-based — not based on what is at any time most politically popular in the Government. The interpellant also raises an important issue of district heating. The Community funds spend billions subsidising district heating pipes in Norwegian cities. In many places this will be a sensible policy. Waste is a resource if we use it correctly. However, if we use the community's resources to bring district heating to passive-household-standard areas, it is likely to be wasted. The government and Enova must therefore have a conscious strategy related to the size of district heating capacity in Norway today and the capacity we will need in the future, while ensuring that district heating does not override better alternatives where they exist. At the same time, the Government must ensure that the district heating industry has framework conditions to meet industrial waste requirements. Eight out of ten waste contracts now go to Sweden, which means that Norwegian district heating plants have to fire with oil rather than waste. It's not very environmentally friendly, as we know. The government should remove the special combustion charge as soon as possible and increase the availability of waste that ends in landfills by doubling the landfill charge. The proposals are free of charge.
